#212. FDA warns of rare eye condition causing blindness from GLP-1 weight loss medications

The federal agency is requiring all GLP-1s to include information about the potential risk on warning labels

The US Food and Drug Administration (FDA) has issued a new warning on GLP-1 medications – which can facilitate weight loss – over a rare eye condition that could lead to sudden and permanent blindness.

On Monday, the FDA announced that it is requiring all GLP-1 medications – including Novo Nordisk’s Ozempic and Wegovy, Eli Lilly’s Mounjaro and Zepbound, as well as Sanofi’s Soliqua – to include information about the potential risk of nonarteritic anterior ischemic optic neuropathy, also known as NAION, on warning labels.

NAION is a rare condition involving sudden reduction of blood flow to the optic nerve, in turn resulting in acute, painless, decreased vision in one eye, including permanent vision loss, the FDA warned.

Common risk factors of NAION include diabetes, smoking, hypertension, as well as hypercholesterolemia. It is the most common cause of acute optic nerve injury in individuals over 50 years old, with an annual average of 2.3 to 10.2 cases out of 100,000 people above that age. There is for now no treatment to reverse vision loss from NAION.

According to the FDA, the agency began looking at potential links between GLP-1s and NAION after a 2024 study published in the journal JAMA Ophthalmology. The study found an increased risk of NAION among patients using GLP-1 medications.

Despite identifying several limitations of the study, the FDA said it “determined that further assessment of the potential increased risk for NAION was warranted”.

The agency proceeded to review 126 clinical trials involving over 134,200 patients and identified 15 probable NAION cases, including 10 among GLP-1 patients; five in patients receiving placebo; and none in patients who were on glucose-lowering medications that were not GLP-1.

The FDA found a NAION incidence rate of 6.4 cases per 100,000 person-years among GLP-1 patients, compared to 3.8 cases among patients on placebo or alternative glucose-lowering medications.

“NAION cases have been reported for all currently marketed GLP-1 RA products,” the FDA said. “FDA concluded that there is a potential increased risk of rare NAION events in patients taking GLP-1 RAs.

“Based on these findings, FDA is requiring that application holders add information about the risk for NAION to the Warnings and Precautions section of labeling of all GLP-1 RAs.”

In response to the FDA announcement, a Novo Nordisk spokesperson told the Guardian that the pharmaceutical company “continues to believe the data do not establish a cause-and-effect relationship and we remain confident in the benefit-risk profile of our GLP-1 medicines.”

The statement also said: “Patient safety is a top priority for Novo, and we continue to monitor the safety of our medicines, review new research and work with health authorities around the world.”

The Guardian has reached out to Eli Lilly and Sanofi for additional comment. Eli Lilly had previously said it is aware of publications on the risk of NAION among GLP-1 patients.

GLP-1 medications are mainly used by people with type 2 diabetes managing their blood sugar – or glucose – levels. Some of the medications are also used to help treat obesity. And some have been known to seek access to such drugs for cosmetic reasons rather than legitimate medical ones.

The FDA’s announcement comes amid warnings from the Netherlands-based non-profit Access to Medicine Foundation that the pharmaceutical focus on GLP-1 weight-loss drugs risks putting global insulin supply at risk.

#211. Proximal Ventures raises debut fund to back startups bringing AI to drug development

While established venture firms continue to attract capital, first-time fund managers are facing one of the toughest fundraising environments in years. But that didn’t stop Sahir Raoof, a former SignalFire investor and executive at two healthcare startups, from raising $27.5 million for his first solo VC fund.

Raoof’s firm, Proximal Ventures, is betting that AI can transform the tools, infrastructure, and services that support drug development.

Pharmaceutical companies have historically outsourced various stages of drug development to specialized service providers, from synthesizing thousands of molecular compounds and figuring out how to manufacture drugs at scale to analyzing clinical trial data.

“For a long time, the assumption was that pharma is not a great buyer of software, but a really good buyer of services,” Raoof told TechCrunch.

Raoof believes AI could automate much of that work, creating an opportunity for startups to capture billions of dollars in pharmaceutical industry spending. By using AI, these companies could deliver the same services at a lower cost and in significantly less time, he said.

Some of the most valuable applications of AI so far have been in service-oriented industries like law and finance, Raoof said. He sees similar opportunities in pharmaceutical services.

Proximal plans to invest in pre-seed and seed-stage startups, writing checks from $500,000 to $1.5 million. The firm has already backed seven startups, including ChronicleBio, co-founded by former OpenAI executive Fidji Simo, which uses AI to analyze biological and medical data to better understand chronic diseases and identify potential treatments.

Before spending two years as a principal at SignalFire, Raoof led business development at clinical trial recruitment startup Iterative Health and previously oversaw partnerships at Flatiron Health, an oncology data company acquired by Roche for $1.9 billion in 2018.

“If you’re building a company that’s selling into the life sciences, one of the hardest things to do is starting that initial commercial flywheel,” Raoof said.

His sales chops, deep understanding of the healthcare ecosystem, and extensive industry relationships gave limited partners, the investors in VC funds, the confidence that he could spot strong startups and help them land customers. Proximal’s backers include VC firms SignalFire and Kleiner Perkins, Bertelsmann Healthcare Investments, and individual investors such as Dimension Capital co-founders Nan Li and Zavain Dar.

Amy Abernethy, co-founder of Highlander Health and former principal deputy commissioner of the FDA, is also an investor. She first met Raoof when they worked together at Flatiron Health, where she served as chief medical officer. She told TechCrunch that his ability to build relationships and spot industry bottlenecks made a lasting impression. Abernethy is convinced that his abilities, along with the understanding of what the healthcare market needs, will make him a successful investor.

It is these qualities that have helped Proximal invest in startups alongside some of the better-known, much larger funds, she added.

#209. Emmanuel and Brigitte Macron file new defamation lawsuit against Candace Owens

Legal battle escalates as far-right US podcaster continues to spread false claims that France’s first lady was born a man

Emmanuel and Brigitte Macron have brought a fresh defamation lawsuit against Candace Owens in her home state of Tennessee, in an escalation of a transatlantic legal battle as the far-right US podcaster continues to spread false claims that France’s first lady was born a man.

The Macrons first sued Owens and her media companies in July 2025 in Delaware in response to what the French first couple called “verifiably false and devastating lies” being repeated by Owens on her podcast and on X. According to that lawsuit, those lies included that Brigitte Macron, 73, was born a man named Jean-Michel Trogneux – who is actually her older brother – and committed identity theft to hide a gender transition; that Emmanuel Macron was elected as French president as part of a CIA mind-control program; and that the couple are blood-related relatives committing incest.

A judge ruled that the Macrons’ claim was limited in scope to Owens’s companies registered in Delaware after she successfully argued that the court’s jurisdiction did not extend to her personally as she did not live in that state. The case against her companies remains pending.

Meanwhile, the new lawsuit filed on 4 November in Nashville, Tennessee, where Owens resides, includes 88 counts of defamation, false light and defamation by implication. They are against Owens personally and build on the Macrons’ original demand for punitive damages against the podcaster on the grounds that she has persisted with the false claims.

“Mrs Owens was adamant that Tennessee is where this case belongs because it’s where she lives,” a representative for the Macrons’ legal team said. “With this filing, we are giving her exactly what she has repeatedly demanded.”

The new complaint stated that since the Delaware lawsuit was filed, Owens had “unequivocally doubled down”, publishing further “outlandish, defamatory, and far-fetched fictions” through her eight-part podcast series called “Becoming Brigitte” – along with accompanying posts on X.

The filing also alleges that Owens – who has millions of followers across her podcast and social media channels – has a financial motive in continuing to peddle the false claims. “She has continued to use her podcast to expand her lies about the Macrons, sell merchandise making fun of the Macrons, and collect donations based on her ‘reporting’ on the Macrons,” the complaint says.

The new lawsuit mentions a “Brigitte doll” that Owens holds in her videos, describing it as “a grotesque, alien-like figure” that is reminiscent of the namesake character in the fictional film ET. The doll “caricatures Mrs Macron’s body”, the lawsuit contends, while adding that Owens’s use of the doll confirms “that her campaign against Mrs Macron is driven by animus toward Mrs Macron’s perceived gender identity, gender, and sexuality”.

The French first couple has experienced “relentless bullying on a worldwide scale” as a result, the lawsuit alleges. It says: “Defendant has subjected the Macrons to a campaign of global humiliation, turning their lives into fodder for profit-driven lies. Owens has dissected their appearance, their marriage, their friends, their family, and their personal history – twisting it all into a grotesque narrative designed to inflame and degrade.”

The Macrons are seeking actual, presumed and punitive damages, in addition to a narrowly tailored injunction barring Owens from republishing any defamatory statements.

“To be clear, there is nothing shameful or defamatory about being a man, being transgender, or being gay,” the lawsuit says. “Owens’ statements are defamatory because they accuse the Macrons of lying to France and to the world, for decades, about who Mrs. Macron is – and of committing crimes to conceal that supposed lie.”

The Guardian has contacted Owens for comment. On X, she wrote in part: “The Macrons have indeed filed a NEW set of claims against me. 88 in total, and this time, they want justice over my Brigitte-Doll.

“Those of you who are fans of the show know that Brigitte-Doll has never pursued the limelight. For her to be called ‘grotesque’ by the French couple is hurtful, to say to the least.

“Brigitte-Doll will be making no further statements at this time.”

#207. Bernie Sanders says he will not back Schumer for Senate Democratic leader

Progressive senator says ‘I like Chuck … but the time is now for change’ as calls grow for new Democratic leadership

Bernie Sanders, a progressive senator from Vermont, said on Tuesday he would not support Chuck Schumer’s bid to serve as the Democratic leader in the US Senate, joining a growing cohort of Democrats calling for a change in leadership.

“I personally have known Chuck Schumer for over 30 years, and I like Chuck. Nobody who works harder than Chuck Schumer. But in my view, the time is now for change, and I will not be supporting Chuck to be re-elected as the Democratic leader,” Sanders told reporters on Capitol Hill.

Sanders, 85, an independent who caucuses with the Democrats, pointed to the results of the midterm elections as proof that voters want to see different leaders in charge.

“If this campaign was about anything, it was about change, the need for the Democratic party, in my view, to move in a new and bold direction,” Sanders said.

Speaking at a post-election conference in Washington on Tuesday, Democratic senator Chris Van Hollen said voters’ desire for change was clear.

“I think the question is whether those calls are being heard within the walls of the Senate Democratic caucus,” Van Hollen said during a live interview at the conference, convened by the progressive organization, Groundwork Collaborative.

Some have pointed to the Maryland senator as a potential successor to Schumer. Van Hollen said he was calling colleagues to gauge their support for leadership change.

While he stopped short of calling for Schumer’s ouster, Van Hollen said the election “should not be coronation”.

“The Senate Democratic caucus needs a little bit of democracy itself in this moment,” he added.

Calls for fresh leadership in the Democratic party have grown louder since 2024, when Joe Biden ended his re-election campaign over concerns about his age. But the party’s sweeping wins in the midterm elections have renewed energy toward enacting change.

Elissa Slotkin, a moderate Democratic senator from battleground Michigan, encouraged anyone interested in leading the Democratic caucus to come forward with a plan.

“I think it is very clear that, on election night, the American people voted for change,” she said, noting that she was not actively seeking the Senate majority leadership position.

A group of high-profile incoming Democratic senators have voiced their desire for new leadership.

Our US morning briefing breaks down the key stories of the day, telling you what’s happening and why it matters

That includes Abdul El-Sayed, the senator-elect from Michigan, who beat a Schumer-backed Democrat to win the party’s nomination, as well as Adam Hamilton, the senator-elect from Kansas, Annie Andrews, the senator-elect from South Carolina, Chris Pappas, the senator-elect from New Hampshire, Troy Jackson, the senator-elect from Maine and Peggy Flanagan, the senator-elect from Minnesota.

But so far, no Democrat has volunteered to take on Schumer, who has served as the leader of the Senate Democrats since 2017.

Sanders told reporters he wants Democrats to elect a new leader through a “process” including “vigorous debate”.

Meanwhile, Schumer has remained confident that he will be re-elected Senate majority leader. The New York senator told reporters last week: “I’ll be re-elected as majority leader.”

He reiterated his confidence on Tuesday, telling reporters: “I’m confident I will be elected majority leader, and our caucus is really united in focusing on bringing down costs, on ending the corruption, on ending the chaos, on ending the war.”

“We have great unity in our caucus and I will be proud to lead it as majority leader,” Schumer said in response to Sanders and Slotkin’s announcements.

#206. OpenAI, Anthropic face new oversight as New York’s AI safety law takes effect

New York is gearing up to enforce one of the country’s most significant AI safety laws. The state opened a registration portal Tuesday, with which large frontier AI developers like Anthropic and OpenAI will be required to register by January 1, 2027.

The RAISE Act, New York’s safety and transparency bill, kicks in at the start of the year. It mandates that large frontier developers — companies with more than $500 million in annual revenue — have to publish safety frameworks on their websites, report critical safety incidents within 72 hours, and issue public model transparency reports.

The newly formed Office of Digital Innovation, Governance, Integrity, and Trust (DIGIT) will implement the RAISE Act and oversee large frontier AI labs. Penalties can be as high as $1 million for one violation and $3 million for subsequent violations.

The state also can bring enforcement actions against companies that fail to register with the new portal, though a spokesperson declined to provide specifics.

“Tech moguls tell us that their vision for AI is inevitable,” Governor Kathy Hochul said Tuesday during a press briefing. “They tell us get on board or get left behind… That any collateral damage is worth it as long as we win the race to the AI cliff.”

On Tuesday, Hochul also launched the New York Center for AI Responsibility and Research based at State University of New York’s Binghamton University. The center will independently evaluate the safety, security, and operation of advanced AI systems and work with DIGIT to review companies’ safety frameworks. Hochul first announced the center in January with $55 million in donations and capital.

“We’re building a national model for smart, sensible AI regulation, a model that puts communities above corporations and people above powerful technology,” Hochul said.

The RAISE Act isn’t the only safety law New York has passed. Last year, the state began enforcing a law that requires AI companion operators to regularly let users know they’re talking to a bot, and to make reasonable efforts to detect suicidal ideation and self-harm.

In October, Hochul signed a separate chatbot safety law for minors, restricting behaviors like deceptive human-like interactions, emotionally manipulative or sycophantic responses, and encouragement of behaviors like suicide or disordered eating. That law takes effect June 1, 2027.

Other states have also passed similar AI safety and transparency bills to the RAISE Act. California’s SB 53, which took effect in January, mandates that frontier AI labs have in place and publish safety frameworks, enact whistleblower protections, and report critical safety incidents within 15 days. Illinois’ SB 315, which will go into effect at the start of 2027, has similar requirements, plus includes independent safety compliance audits.

#205. Israeli military veterans, a backbone of protest movement, vow to keep demonstrating

In a sea of Israeli flags, Yiftach Golov holds one that looks a little different.

Among the hundreds of thousands of protesters who took to the streets for the 13th week in a row on Saturday, Golov hoists a brown flag that represents a group called “Brother and Sisters in Arms.”

They are veterans – many, like Golov, from elite forces – who now feel they are fighting on a new battlefield: To save Israeli democracy.

“We believe this is our responsibility to go once again called to the flag of the nation to stop this madness to defend Israel,” Golov said, as he weaved his way through the protesters on Tel Aviv’s Kaplan street, between the high-rises that house many of Israel’s high tech companies.

During the second intifada, in the early 2000s, Golov served in a special forces reconnaissance unit. He was never before particularly political, focusing more on getting his PhD in biophysics from Tel Aviv University.

But when the protest movement against the Israeli government’s judicial overhaul plan began in January, Golov attended one a demonstration and soon became one of thousands of veterans, and now military reservists, who have taken up the cause as their new mission.

Some, including elite Air Force reservists, have taken it a step further, threatening not to heed the call to train or even serve in protest of the government’s plans planned judicial changes, which would give the governing parties more control over Israel’s judiciary.

Others have taken to becoming some of the most active organizers and demonstrators. Last week, a group from Brothers and Sisters in Arms protested by carrying a figure wrapped in the Israeli flag on a stretcher, the way they would carry a wounded comrade off the field.

While Golov says he has not taken the drastic step to refuse service, he understands the motivation.

“We’re fighting for justice and liberty, just like the American story, that’s the values that that are being represented symbolized back when we look at our flag, that’s something that was lacking lacking for the last few decades. So basically, we reclaim the flag,” he said.

Palestinian taxi driver recounts surviving attack by right-wing protesters

Fellow members of the group, all wearing brown shirts with the organization’s logo, come up and say hello. They’re sprinkled all throughout the protests. One is even leading the “Pink Front,” a group of coordinated drummers who look like they are dressed for a rave, and often lead the chants at the protests.

They’re using skills they learned in the military – how to organize, how to mobilize – now for the protests. But more importantly, they say they have the same type of motivation.

“The very deep feeling that you are part of something bigger than yourself, that (you’re) allowed to sacrifice anything that is needed, whether it’s your career, health, seriously mental health,” Golov said. “We all have a mission, you’re willing to do it at any cost. You’re very determined, you know that you are on the right side, you’re carrying the torch of light. That keeps us being highly motivated despite the fact that we’re not sleeping for days.”

Israel’s protest movement is made up of many disparate groups, but the pressure from Israel’s much vaunted veterans has been seen as a key to moving the needle.

Last Monday, after weeks of sustained protests and the largest general strike in Israeli history, Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu announced a pause to the legislation, to allow time for negotiations with the opposition.

But despite the announcements, protesters are still out in the streets in large numbers. CNN affiliate Channel 12 in Israel estimated the size of Saturday’s demonstration in Tel Aviv at about 150,000 people. Organizers claimed it was 230,000.

Last week’s mass protests and widespread strike action came after Netanyahu said he had decided to fire Defense Minister Yoav Gallant for advocating a delay in passing the legislation – a move that Netanyahu has since delayed, sources told CNN, due to “the present security situation.”

In his televised speech calling for a delay, Gallant had said the pause in the legislation was needed “for the security of Israel,” citing the refusal of some Israel Defense Forces reservists to train in protest of the government plans. He said pressing ahead with the proposals could threaten Israel’s security.

Under pressure at home and from allies abroad, Netanyahu said he would delay votes on the remaining legislation until after the Knesset’s Passover recess in April “to give time for a real chance for a real debate.”

“Out of the responsibility to the nation, I decided to delay … the vote, in order to give time for discussion,” he added.

But Netanyahu indicated that the delay was only temporary. He insisted that the overhaul was necessary, and reiterated criticism of refusal to train or serve in the military in protest at the planned changes. “Refusing is the end of our country,” he said.

Many protesters don’t believe that the pause is real, or say it’s simply a stalling tactic to give Netanyahu some breathing room and get the protesters to go home before he plows on with the reforms.

“We will start doing deactivation only when we will know 100% that Israel state will stay a functional democratic country. Whatever needs to be done for that,” Golov said.

#202. Republicans elect Jim Jordan as House minority leader after midterm defeats

Republicans pick Jordan as House leader and John Thune as Senate leader for last two years of Trump’s presidency

Republicans have elected veteran congressman Jim Jordan as their new leader in the House of Representatives, putting a strident defender of Donald Trump at the helm of their conference as they navigate the fallout from sweeping losses in last week’s midterm elections.

Jordan will serve as minority leader under the new Congress that begins next year, where Democrats will have their largest margins in both the House and Senate in years. He will be responsible for shaping the party’s strategy against the waves of investigations Democrats have promised to open into Trump’s second administration, as well as their vows of an ambitious legislative agenda, regardless of whether it has the president’s support.

“Over the next two years, House Republicans will fight for American families,” Jordan said in brief remarks to reporters after his elections. “When Democrats offer more government, we’ll offer more freedom, and we will bring that fight in committee, on the floor, and on the stump every single day.”

The Ohio lawmaker is a longtime leader of the right wing in the US House, and gained national renown for standing up for Trump against earlier efforts by Democrats to hold him to account. In 2023, Jordan was briefly a candidate for speaker after dissident Republicans removed Kevin McCarthy as the chamber’s leader, but his bid collapsed after it became clear he did not have enough support.

House Republicans elected Jordan over Steve Scalise, who serves as majority leader in the current Congress. Following comprehensive defeats last week in the midterm elections, the current House speaker, Mike Johnson, announced he would not remain in leadership, and has thus far not committed to serving out the rest of his term.

“Jim was a mentor of mine when I first came to Congress 10 years ago, and we worked alongside one another all these years,” Johnson said. “He’s always been regarded as a leader by the conference, and he’s going to do an extraordinary job.”

Jordan will serve alongside John Thune, the Republican Senate leader who also won re-election from his conference on Tuesday.

“I’m proud of what this Republican team has accomplished together, and we remain united in our commitment to advancing conservative priorities, working with President Trump and our colleagues in the House, and holding the line against Democrats’ efforts to enact their far-left, liberal agenda,” Thune said in a statement.

Elections for both roles were held in behind-closed-door meetings.

Ahead of the House GOP’s meeting, several spoke of the need for a change in strategy, after two years spent trying to answer Trump’s demands with one of the smallest majorities in history, only to have voters decisively remove them from power.

“I want somebody that’s going to message and admit that … we made mistakes,” said Tim Burchett, a conservative lawmaker from Tennessee, who declined to say who he would be voting for.

Florida congresswoman Anna Paulina Luna said she was backing Jordan, and blamed Scalise for not fighting hard enough to get rightwing priorities through the chamber.

“If you have a play and you get destroyed, you don’t put the same people back in charge that were part of running that failed play,” she said.

First elected in 2006, Jordan helped found the rightwing House Freedom Caucus in 2015, and served as its first chair. Seen almost always in shirtsleeves, his profile rose during Trump’s first term, particularly after Democrats regained control of Congress’s lower chamber in the 2018 midterms. As the top Republican on the oversight and then the judiciary committees, Jordan made energetic stands against efforts by Democrats to investigate Trump, and served as a member of the president’s defense team during his first impeachment.

Jordan echoed Trump’s false claims of fraud in the 2020 election, and championed the investigation of Hunter Biden during Joe Biden’s final two years in office. Since Trump returned to the White House, Jordan has used his role as judiciary committee chair to pursue allegations of “weaponization” by prosecutors who investigated Trump, including Jack Smith, the special counsel who oversaw the federal cases.

Democrats decried Jordan’s appointment as sign that the party was doubling down on extremism, with Virginia congressman Don Beyer noting that Jordan’s election came days after Trump had named immigration hardliner Stephen Miller as his chief of staff.

“Republicans learned absolutely nothing from what happened a week ago, and are responding by elevating their most extreme voices to positions of power,” he said.

#201. OpenAI acquires Odessia, travel startup created by Sonder founder

OpenAI has acquired an AI travel agent called Odessia, according to Odessia’s founder, Francis Davidson. Davidson says in a post announcing the deal that he’s now working for OpenAI, helping the AI lab with the future of travel.

Terms of the deal were not disclosed, but this looks like an acqui-hire. Davidson’s announcement focused largely on how he built his AI travel agent Odessia by directing teams of AI agents. The year-old Odessia raised a $6 million pre-seed round led by Sequoia and launched its AI travel agent around five months ago. Davidson says that it had helped early adopters plan millions of dollars’ worth of travel, but the tool has already been shut down.

An OpenAI spokesperson confirmed the hire to TechCrunch. “We’re thrilled to welcome Francis to OpenAI. Francis is a proven founder who has built multiple businesses to help people travel, and we’re excited he’s chosen to build with us.”

However the AI lab has not announced new travel-specific products or plans. It does, however, count such companies as Bookings.com, Expedia, and KAYAK as customers or partners. Travel is also one of the main areas that people are using personal AI assistants to help with. And, back in July 2025, when OpenAI launched its ChatGPT agent, it mentioned travel as one of its potential uses.

OpenAI’s spokesperson didn’t have more to share on its plans for travel-related products, except to say that travel is one of the areas that chatbot users frequently ask about. “1.2 billion people turn to ChatGPT each week for help with everyday tasks — from understanding their health, figuring out what to make for dinner, building a budget, and even planning a trip. We see a real opportunity to make these experiences even more useful,” the spokesperson said.

Acquiring Odessia is interesting for another reason. Davidson is well known in the travel industry after he founded a travel startup called Sonder as a college student in 2014. It was sort of like Airbnb, but it focused on short-term leasing of upscale apartments in major cities worldwide. The pandemic badly hurt it, and as it climbed out of that rut, it went public via a SPAC but didn’t raise as much money as it needed, Davidson later said in a post announcing his resignation as CEO in June 2025.

When a key partnership with Marriott fell through, the company went into bankruptcy and wound down. The bankruptcy trustee sold Sonder’s name, trademarks, and website domain in 2026 to TravelAI, and that website operates as a curated guide to the kind of travel rental properties that Sonder was once known for. Prior to its downfall, Davidson had built Sonder into a unicorn with $143 million in revenues. While Sonder was not, ultimately, a success story, Davidson is the kind of battle-scarred founder that Silicon Valley often values.

So we’ll have to wait and see what OpenAI has in store now with travel, now that he’s joined the company.

Jordan, the hardline conservative from Ohio, beat fellow congressman Steve Scalise by a vote of 106-73; Thune won re-election without a challenger

Republicans elect Jim Jordan as House minority leader after party’s midterm defeats

Congressman Jim Jordan, a Republican hardliner from Ohio, has won the election to become the next House minority leader in Congress.

Jordan beat Representative Steve Scalise, the current majority leader, by 106-73 in a closed-door vote to lead Republicans from January. He replaces the current House speaker, Mike Jonson, who said he would not run when the GOP cedes control to Democrats in the new year.

A new Reuters analysis shows that the Securities and Exchange Commission brought the fewest enforcement actions in fiscal year 2026 in 35-years.

The SEC said Tuesday it brought 348 enforcement actions in 2026, the lowest number of cases since 1991. According to Reuters, in 2025 the agency brought 456 cases – the lowest number since 2008. The SEC oversees more than 30,000 brokers, funds and other financial firms.

“The new Democratic majority in Congress will closely scrutinize ​whether [current chair Paul Atkins’] SEC is protecting the American people – or working for scammers and Trump’s billionaire buddies on Wall Street,” senator Elizabeth Warren, who is seen as likely to chair the Senate Banking Committee next year, said in a statement about the enforcement slowdown.

The Commodity Futures Trading Commission privately notified Kalshi of the enforcement activity.

Kalshi spokesperson Elisabeth Diana said in a statement to Politico that the CFTC’s warning “is about a procedural error involving the submission of a handful of contracts, many of which weren’t live” and added “We are already addressing the issue.”

Donald Trump was told in May that the United States had obtained sensitive intelligence that Jensen Huang, the CEO of Nvidia, was in a relationship with a woman believed to be a Chinese spy, the New York Times reports, citing people with knowledge of the matter.

Secretary of state Marco Rubio shared the news with Trump on board Air Force One while the president was en route to China, shortly before Huang joined the president.

The news directly contradicts the president’s assertion during a press conference yesterday, when he told reporters “No, I didn’t hear about it then” in response to a question about whether Trump knew about the relationship at the time of his trip to China.

At a hearing about an FCC commissioner’s nomination, Lujan said Carr “has been evading questions and ⁠ignoring his responsibilities.”

“I don’t think chairman Carr is going ‌to be around more than another three or four months,” Lujan added.

Asked for comment, a spokesperson for Carr told Reuters, “Over the next two years, under Chairman Carr’s leadership, the FCC will continue to deliver great results for the American people.”

With the Pan American Health Organization expected to decide whether the United States can keep its measles elimination status this week, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention tells Reuters it will not fundamentally change its response to measles outbreaks.

“Let’s say we did lose our measles elimination status. We’re going to be doing the same ⁠exact thing that we’ve been doing today, which is working with the states,” CDC director Erica Schwartz told Reuters today.

The CDC will make sure “we are ​leaning in to help them provide awareness about measles, emphasizing, reemphasizing ‌that the number one way to ‌prevent measles is the MMR (combined measles, mumps and rubella) vaccine,” she said.

The US achieved its measles-free status in 2000. This year, the CDC has logged 5,182 confirmed measles cases so far – up from 2,289 cases in all of 2025, 285 cases in 2024, and 59 cases in 2023.

Health secretary Robert F Kennedy Jr, a leading vaccination skeptic, has spent years amplifying misinformation about vaccines, including the shot that protects against measles, mumps and rubella.

Here’s a bit more background on John Thune, the Republican senator for South Dakota who has won re-election to serve as Senate minority leader in the next Congress.

Thune was first elected Senate majority leader in 2024, to fill the position left vacant by Mitch McConnell. While that year he faced competition from John Cornyn of Texas and Rick Scott of Florida, he ran uncontested in his bid for Senate leadership this year.

Thune had previously been a critic of Donald Trump and called his role in the 6 January 2021 attack on the US Capitol “inexcusable”. Trump responded by calling him a “Rino” (Republican in name only) and called for him to face a primary challenge in his 2022 bid for re-election to the Senate. But the two reconciled, and Thune endorsed Trump’s 2024 presidential candidacy.

He replaces the current House speaker, Mike Jonson, who said he would not run when the GOP cedes control to Democrats in the new year.

Jordan, 62, who currently serves as chair of the House judiciary committee, is known as a pugnacious representative and fierce ally of Donald Trump. Meanwhile, Republicans in the upper chamber, re-elected John Thune to serve as Senate minority leader in the next Congress. The GOP senator from North Dakota didn’t face a challenger in the closed-door vote, as Democrats prepare for at least a 10-seat majority in the new year. “I appreciated the opportunity to have an open, honest, and transparent conversation with my colleagues today, and I am honored to have their continued trust and support,” Thune said in a statement, after Senate Republicans met on Capitol Hill today to strategize after their drubbing in the recent midterm elections.

It has been 150 years since the last US defense secretary was impeached. That was William Belknap in 1876, who served under President Ulysses S Grant. Belknap was eventually acquitted by the Senate.

Top Democrat Chuck Schumer said he is “confident” that he has enough votes to become Senate majority leader in the next Congress.

Slotkin added today that the intra-party battle is not between progressive and moderate, but rather “team fight versus team flight”.

She added: “Do you actually want to fight hard for the things that are going to help people, or do you kind of want to just sit there and look like you’re doing something?”

#194. A new approach to a Covid-19 nasal vaccine shows early promise

Scientists in Germany say they’ve been able to make a nasal vaccine that can shut down a Covid-19 infection in the nose and throat, where the virus gets its first foothold in the body.

In experiments in hamsters, two doses of the vaccine – which is made with a live but weakened form of the coronavirus that causes Covid-19 – blocked the virus from copying itself in the animals’ upper airways, achieving “sterilizing immunity” and preventing illness, a long-sought goal of the pandemic.

WHO experts revise Covid-19 vaccine advice, say healthy kids and teens low risk

Although this vaccine has several more hurdles to clear before it gets to a doctor’s office or drug store, other nasal vaccines are in use or are nearing the finish line in clinical trials.

China and India both rolled out vaccines given through the nasal tissues last fall, though it’s not clear how well they may be working. Studies on the effectiveness of these vaccines have yet to be published, leaving much of the world to wonder whether this approach to protection really works in people.

The US has reached something of a stalemate with Covid-19. Even with the darkest days of the pandemic behind us, hundreds of Americans are still dying daily as the infection continues to simmer in the background of our return to normal life.

As long as the virus continues to spread among people and animals, there’s always the potential for it mutate into a more contagious or more damaging version of itself. And while Covid infections have become manageable for most healthy people, they may still pose a danger to vulnerable groups such as the elderly and immunocompromised.

Researchers hope next-generation Covid-19 vaccines, which aim to shut down the virus before it ever gets a chance to make us sick and ultimately prevent the spread of infection, could make our newest resident respiratory infection less of a threat.

China approves its first mRNA Covid-19 vaccine

One way scientists are trying to do that is by boosting mucosal immunity, beefing up immune defenses in the tissues that line the upper airways, right where the virus would land and begin to infect our cells.

It’s a bit like stationing firefighters underneath the smoke alarm in your house, says study author Emanuel Wyler, a scientist at the Max Delbruck Center for Molecular Medicine in the Helmholtz Association in Berlin.

The immunity that’s created by shots works throughout the body, but it resides primarily in the blood. That means it may take longer to mount a response.

“If they are already on site, they can immediately eliminate the fire, but if they’re like 2 miles away, they first need to drive there, and by that time, one-third of the house is already in full flames,” Wyler said.

Mucosal vaccines are also better at priming a different kind of first responder than injections do. They do a better job of summoning IgA antibodies, which have four arms to grab onto invaders instead of the two arms that the y-shaped IgG antibodies have. Some scientists think IgA antibodies may be less picky about their targets than IgG antibodies, which makes them better equipped to deal with new variants.

The new nasal vaccine takes a new approach to a very old idea: weakening a virus so it’s no longer a threat and then giving it to people so their immune systems can learn to recognize and fight it off. The first vaccines using this approach date to the 1870s, against anthrax and rabies. Back then, scientists weakened the agents they were using with heat and chemicals.

The researchers manipulated the genetic material in the virus to make it harder for cells to translate. This technique, called codon pair deoptimization, hobbles the virus so it can be shown to the immune system without making the body sick.

“You could imagine reading a text … and every letter is a different font, or every letter is a different size, then the text is much harder to read. And this is basically what we do in codon pair deoptimization,” Wyler said.

In the hamster studies, which were published Monday in the journal Nature Microbiology, two doses of the live but weakened nasal vaccine created a much stronger immune response than either two doses of an mRNA-based vaccine or one that uses an adenovirus to ferry the vaccine instructions into cells.

The researchers think the live weakened vaccine probably worked better because it closely mimics the process of a natural infection.

The nasal vaccine also previews the entire coronavirus for the body, not just its spike proteins like current Covid-19 vaccines do, so the hamsters were able to make immune weapons against a wider range of targets.

As promising as all this sounds, vaccine experts say caution is warranted. This vaccine still has to pass more tests before it’s ready for use, but they say the results look encouraging.

Health officials consider another round of bivalent boosters for the most vulnerable Americans

“They did a very nice job. This is obviously a competent and thoughtful team that did this work, and impressive in the scope of what they did. Now it just needs to be repeated,” perhaps in primates and certainly in humans before it can be widely used, said Dr. Greg Poland, who designs vaccines at the Mayo Clinic. He was not involved in the new research.

The study began in 2021, before the Omicron variant was around, so the vaccine tested in these experiments was made with the original strain of the coronavirus. In the experiments, when they infected animals with Omicron, the live but weakened nasal vaccine still performed better than the others, but its ability to neutralize the virus was diminished. Researchers think it will need an update.

It also needs to be tested in humans, and Wyler says they’re working on that. The scientists have partnered with a Swiss company called RocketVax to start phase I clinical trials.

Other vaccines are further along, but the progress has been “slow and halting,” Poland said. Groups working on these vaccines are struggling to raise the steep costs of getting a new vaccine to market, and they’re doing it in a setting where people tend to think the vaccine race has been won and done.

In reality, Poland said, we’re far from that. All it would take is another Omicron-level shift in the evolution of the virus, and we could be back at square one, with no effective tools against the coronavirus.

“That’s foolish. We should be developing a pan-coronavirus vaccine that does induce mucosal immunity and that is long-lived,” he said.

At least four nasal vaccines for Covid-19 have reached late-stage testing in people, according to the World Health Organization’s vaccine tracker.

The nasal vaccines in use in China and India rely on harmless adenoviruses to ferry their instructions into cells, although effectiveness data for these has not been published.

Two other nasal vaccines are finishing human studies.

Covid vaccine maker Novavax says it may not survive

One, a recombinant vaccine that can be produced cheaply in chicken eggs, the same way many flu vaccines are, is being put through its paces by researchers at Mount Sinai in New York City.

Another, like the German vaccine, uses a live but weakened version of the virus. It’s being developed by a company called Codagenix. Results of those studies, which were carried out in South America and Africa, may come later this year.

The German team says it’s eagerly watching for the Codagenix data.

“They will be very important in order to know where whether this kind of attempt is basically promising or not,” Wyler said.

They have reason to worry. Respiratory infections have proved to be tough targets for inhaled vaccines.

FluMist, a live but weakened form of the flu virus, works reasonably well in children but doesn’t help adults as much. The reason is thought to be that adults already have immune memory for the flu, and when the virus is injected into the nose, the vaccine mostly boosts what’s already there.

Sign up here to get The Results Are In with Dr. Sanjay Gupta every Tuesday from the CNN Health team.

Still, some of the most potent vaccines such as the vaccine against measles, mumps and rubella use live attenuated viruses, so it’s a promising approach.

Another consideration is that live vaccines can’t be taken by everyone. People with very compromised immunity are often cautioned against using live vaccines because even these very weakened viruses may be risky for them.

“Although it’s strongly attenuated, it’s still a real virus,” Wyler said, so it would have to be used carefully.

#190. Woman jailed for posing as man to dupe partner into sex found guilty of similar deception

Jury finds Blade Silvano, who presented herself as a male army veteran, guilty of six counts of sexual assault

A woman serving a prison sentence for sexual assault after pretending to be a man and duping a woman into a physical relationship has been found guilty of tricking another woman into a similar abusive partnership.

In both cases, Blade Silvano, 44, met the women on an online dating site and falsely claimed she was a man who had served in the armed forces before meeting them in person and sexually assaulting them.

Both women said Silvano tricked them into believing she was male and neither had any idea they were being deceived.

Silvano was jailed in 2023 in Cambridge after a jury heard she carried out the deception using a blindfold, sex toy and a “wholly fake persona”. Silvano and her victim even became engaged as a man and woman.

On Tuesday, a jury at Caernarfon crown court in north Wales found her guilty of six counts of sexual assault by penetration between November 2022 and the following June after being told she had carried out the same sort of deception on another woman.

Using the alias “Hunter”, Silvano began messaging the victim on the Plenty of Fish site before meeting her for sex, always keeping the lights very low and not allowing herself to be touched intimately.

Silvano claimed to be a captain and surgeon in the army and on one occasion claimed “his” condom had broken as they had sex. Silvano assured the alleged victim it was “very unlikely” she would get pregnant and told her: “Trust me, I’m a doctor.”

The jury was told the woman noticed her partner sounded a “little feminine” and began to have doubts about their “odd” sex life.

Eventually, the victim received a call from Silvano’s long-term partner, a woman, who said the person she’d known as “Hunter” was female, the court heard.

The victim told police she felt sick when she learned of the deception, saying: “I thought it was some sort of joke.” In court, she said: “I believed I was in a relationship with a straight, heterosexual man. I would never go out with a woman.”

Philip Clemo, prosecuting, told the jury Silvano was a gifted con artist and suggested she was inspired by the myth of Zeus turning into a bull.

The Cambridgeshire offences took place in 2016 and 2017. Using the fake name Blade Mendez, Silvano presented herself as a male army veteran throughout a two-year relationship.

She would send the victim photos of herself apparently shaving her face and would pretend to urinate standing up.

Silvano, of Shropshire, would not allow the victim to touch her genitals, claiming to be “body conscious due to injuries sustained in the army”. Silvano and the woman got engaged and she was encouraged to try on wedding dresses and book a registrar.

The victim accidentally uncovered Silvano’s deception after coming across her on Facebook in September 2018 “in a completely different living situation”. Silvano was found guilty of two counts of assault by penetration.

Silvano will be sentenced for the six sexual assaults next month. Judge Jones ordered a pre-sentence report so that the risk she posed could be assessed.

Information and support for anyone affected by rape or sexual abuse issues is available from the following organisations. In the UK, Rape Crisis offers support on 0808 500 2222 in England and Wales, 0808 801 0302 in Scotland, or 0800 0246 991 in Northern Ireland. In the US, Rainn offers support on 800-656-4673. In Australia, support is available at 1800Respect (1800 737 732). Other international helplines can be found at ibiblio.org/rcip/internl.html

#187. The $500 billion beauty industry's 'green' ambitions are a patchwork at best. And they're falling short

The escalating climate crisis is shifting many people’s purchasing patterns and this extends to the $500 billion dollar global beauty industry which is grappling with a range of sustainability challenges across product manufacturing, packaging and disposal.

'Delay means death': We're running out of ways to adapt to the climate crisis, new report shows. Here are the key takeaways

Strategy and consulting firm Simon Kucher’s Global Sustainability Study 2021 found 60% of consumers around the world rated sustainability as an important purchase criterion, and 35% were willing to pay more for sustainable products or services.

This shift in consumer preferences has propelled many beauty brands to set environmental goals: to move away from single-use and virgin plastics, provide recyclable, reusable and refillable packaging and offer more transparency around products’ ingredients so customers can ascertain how “green” their purchase is.

However, consumers still struggle to understand the sustainability credentials of many products, according to the British Beauty Council. This is because the industry’s clean-up efforts have been inconsistent, and fall short of making a recognizable impact in the absence of collective goal-setting, global strategy and standardized regulations.

There is no international standard for the beauty industry on how much product ingredient information to share with customers — or how to do so. Brands can set their own rules and goals, giving rise to confusion and “greenwashing,” where sustainability claims are often touted but not substantiated.

Companies often use marketing language like “clean beauty” to make it seem like their products are natural, for example, when they may not actually be organic, sustainable or ethically made.

After rebranding in the West, many beauty companies are still offering to 'whiten' skin elsewhere

“The term ‘clean beauty’ has become quite dangerous. It’s used to sell more products,” according to British Beauty Council CEO Millie Kendall, who added that such buzzwords are losing traction in the UK as British customers wise up to their shortcomings. “Customers need better marketing information and certification information.”

In a 2021 report calling on the industry to have “the courage to change” their business practices, the British Beauty Council wrote that, all too often, even natural ingredients involved in manufacturing products give way to “over-consumption, non-regenerative farming practices, pollution, waste and neglect.”

“The only way out of this is transparency,” Kendall told CNN.

Jen Lee, chief impact officer at US-based brand Beautycounter, said she continues to see confusion over ingredients among consumers. (In 2013, the company launched and published “The Never List,” which currently cites more than 2,800 chemicals — including heavy metals, parabens and formaldehyde — it claims to never use in its products.)

“Natural vs. synthetic ingredients has been a conversation. People think natural is safer, but it’s not always the case,” Lee explained. “Natural ingredients formulated in the industry can have toxic load. Heavy metals can occur in natural components of the earth.”

Mother loses peripheral vision from apparent exposure to mercury in beauty creams. Toxic levels in her home put family at risk, say experts

“We used to be more natural and organic,” added Sasha Plavsic, founder of makeup brand ILIA Beauty. “What was challenging is (that) raw materials were difficult to source or would come in inconsistently or products wouldn’t perform.”

Most makeup is created and molded at high temperatures, Plavsic explained. Purely organic materials often fall apart in this heat, leading to inconsistent results and subpar product performance. “Not every synthetic is bad,” Plavsic said. “Sometimes, it helps create the best in class formula.”

The industry’s plastic packaging is a particular sustainability challenge — 95% is thrown away and the vast majority is not recycled, according to the British Beauty Council.

The cosmetics business is the fourth biggest plastic packaging user globally — after food and beverage, industrial packaging and pharmaceuticals — and plastic is about 67% of the industry’s packaging volume, according to Vantage Market Research. Beauty giant L’Oreal used 144,430 metric tons of plastic in its packaging material in 2021, for example, according to the Ellen Macarthur Foundation (EMF). Estee Lauder Companies reported its brands produced 71,600 metric tons of plastic in product packaging that same year.

And only 9% of the global plastic waste is recycled, according to a report from the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development. The United States only recycles 4% of its plastic waste.

Single-use plastic is wreaking havoc on the planet. Here's what you can do to minimize your impact

Many brands are trying to phase out harmful plastics from their operations and adopt post-consumer recycled (PCR) plastic. (L’Oreal has set a target of 50% PCR plastic usage by 2025, while Estee Lauder is targeting 25% “or more” PCR plastic — but both are far from achieving their targets.)

“Between 60-70 major global brands have made unprecedented progress” in PCR plastic usage across industries, EMF’s Plastic Initiative Lead Sander DeFruyt told CNN. But DeFruyt stressed that PCR plastic must be adopted in conjunction with brands removing single and virgin plastics from their usage cycles to truly make a difference.

However, PCR plastic is not easy to find — low recycling rates around the world mean there is limited supply. Meanwhile, demand for it is growing demand across industries, DeFruyt said. This competition hikes up its price, which is already higher than virgin plastic.

Hair care brand FEKKAI claims that it used up to 95% PCR content in its packaging, but pricing and supply issues posed a challenge, forcing it to currently aim for containers and packaging that feature at least 50% PCR in its packaging.

“PCR plastic is more expensive than stock plastic. The cost is hard and then sourcing it is too,” founder Frédéric Fekkai told CNN. “PCR is close to our heart, but there is a massive demand, so finding recycled plastic is difficult.”

Beauty retailers plays a pivotal — and under-utilized — role, with control over stocking decisions and supply chains. But many vary when it comes to the standards they set for brands they sell.

“Smaller businesses do more, full stop,” said Jessi Baker, founder of the technology platform Provenance, which helps brands display their sustainability credentials for customers. “They move more nimbly. Some of them are born-good brands — climate friendliness was part of their setup. They don’t need to restructure their entire supply chain. Their culture already has it compared to the larger brands who need to work hard to change.”

A history of beauty trends -- and the standards that shaped them

Sephora launched its “Clean + Planet Positive” initiative in 2021, which labeled products that met its set criteria. (This is separate from the French retailer’s “Clean at Sephora” program, which is currently facing a consumer lawsuit alleging it carries a significant percentage of products understood by customers to be harmful.) Target launched a similar program in 2022, featuring a “Target Zero” icon for both online and in-store offerings that either have reusable, recyclable, compostable or reduced plastic packaging, or feature waterless or concentrated products.

Still, many steps taken by brands and retailers do not even begin to touch on the waste and pollution generated throughout supply chains, manufacturing and shipping, all huge problems for the industry to grapple with.

The gaps in standardization in the beauty ecosystem can, to some extent, be filled by certifications such as the US-born B Corporation, or B Corp. This accreditation, one of the most well-known in the beauty space, is issued by the non-profit B Lab, which scores a company on a variety of criteria around ethics and sustainability. However beneficial it may be among eco-conscious consumers, though, it is currently completely voluntary for brands to apply for.

Governments and multinationals enforcing regulations and setting a base line for brands to operate from when making sustainability claims would go a long way to making change, many experts and business leaders believe.

Susanne Kaufmann, founder of her namesake beauty brand, says her efforts in Austria would reap better results if more countries around the world had stricter, more uniform garbage disposal laws.

A trash heap 62 meters high shows the scale of India's climate challenge

“I package our product in a recyclable material,” Kaufmann said. (Her products’ packaging, which is refillable and reusable, is made from 75% recycled plastic — and is 100% recyclable.) If I send this to the US, the garbage is not separated… and it’s not recyclable,” she explained, referring to inconsistencies in recycling laws across the United States.

And when it comes to ingredients, the European Chemicals Agency lists 2,495 substances banned from use in cosmetic products marketed for sale or use in the bloc. But the US Food and Drug administration only lists 11, making it more challenging for American consumers to find safer, greener options. The Environmental Working Group, a non-profit watchdog, studied lab tests of 51 sunscreen products in 2021 and found that only 35% of products met the EU standard, compared with 94% that passed the US standard.

However, while government can set minimum requirements, Mia Davis, vice president of sustainability and impact at beauty retailer Credo Beauty, says the needle will move in the private sector.

Sustainable ships: The world's most eco-conscious cruises

“Regulation can raise the floor a bit. A person who doesn’t know about any (sustainability issues) should still be able to walk into a bodega and get clean products… But that’s never going to be what the market can do,” she said. “Market leadership is key.”

In the absence of bold regulations or global standards on sustainability practices, this “leadership” — undertaken both by brands and customers in the beauty marketplace — is likely to be the most immediately impactful vector for addressing the industry’s climate shortcomings. It will take continued collective advocacy and initiative to see meaningful climate-conscious change.

#185. Judge who sentenced Russell Brand praised for dispelling rape myths

Criminal Bar Association and campaigners hail Mr Justice Bennathan for delivering remarks with ‘piercing clarity’

The judge who sentenced Russell Brand has been praised for dispelling rape myths with “piercing clarity”.

Mr Justice Bennathan’s sentencing remarks were particularly welcomed given that rape myths had played a part in the trial. He was also lauded for calling out “enablers” who witnessed sexual assaults by the comedian.

Sentencing Brand to 10 and a half years on Monday for raping a woman and sexually assaulting three others, Bennathan said: “Whatever women wear, wherever they go, indeed whatever they drink, they retain the right to say no and to demand justice from the criminal courts when sexual predators like you, Russell Brand, act without their consent.”

Andrew Thomas KC, the chair of the Criminal Bar Association, and Kama Melly KC, its vice-chair, said: “We welcome the piercing clarity of the judge’s statement. Myths about rape and sexual assault must play no part in our culture or in our courtrooms. Raping or sexually assaulting someone is a crime, and blame lies entirely with the perpetrator.

“Victims of sexual violence should know that when they go to the prosecution authorities, their evidence will be judged fairly and properly. As the verdicts in Russell Brand’s case show, a properly directed jury will not be put off by a defendant’s appeal to rape myths.”

Ciara Bergman, the chief executive of Rape Crisis England & Wales, said Bennathan’s remarks were “hugely important” at the end of such a high-profile trial. “We’re pleased he emphasised and upheld women’s rights and freedoms, and recognised the profound courage of the women who came forward, and what they endured during the trial, too,” she said.

“This recognition stands in stark contrast to the appalling accusations made by Brand’s defence barrister, who perpetuated myths, misconceptions and conceptions about rape, such as needing to have fought, screamed or reported immediately for it to be considered a credible allegation. Of course this is not the case; there are a range of ways in which people respond to the threat of an imminent sexual assault and all of them are valid.”

Deniz Uğur, the deputy director of the End Violence Against Women Coalition, said rape myths should be prohibited. “The judge’s comments are welcome given the significant role that rape myths played in this trial, which in turn have influenced public discussions about the case,” she said. “This will be having a profound effect not only on the women involved in this case, but on survivors exposed to victim-blaming and misconceptions online and in their daily lives.”

On Tuesday, Labour’s deputy leader, Lucy Powell, praised Bennathan’s “incredibly powerful” sentencing remarks, contrasting them with the “depressing” nature of the cross-examination of Brand’s victims by his barrister, Ian Winter KC.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

The judge also took aim at Brand’s “enablers” who witnessed Brand sexually assault women in front of them at a radio station and TV studio. He said of the former incident: “No doubt your companions thought it was outrageous laddish behaviour that only you could get away with. It was outrageous and possibly laddish, but also a criminal sexual offence, and as it turns out you haven’t got away with it.”

Bennathan told Brand the assault at the TV studio showed “your staggering arrogance, and it shows how your entourage indulged your dreadful behaviour”.

Bergman described the comments as “crucial”, adding: “This case and the national response to it is about more than just one man, it is about everyone who laughed along, didn’t speak up and/or looked away, even when it was happening right in front of them. They may not have committed Brand’s crimes, but they contributed to the culture that meant he went on harming other women with impunity for so long.”

Uğur similarly welcomed Bennathan’s remarks, saying it was “critically important to look at the many institutions and individuals that enabled [Brand’s] behaviour”.

The investigation is not linked to any other ongoing counter-terrorism policing investigations.

#180. Hundreds of millions at risk from Chinese shopping app malware

It is one of China’s most popular shopping apps, selling clothing, groceries and just about everything else under the sun to more than 750 million users a month.

But according to cybersecurity researchers, it can also bypass users’ cell phone security to monitor activities on other apps, check notifications, read private messages and change settings.

And once installed, it’s tough to remove.

In a detailed investigation, CNN spoke to half a dozen cybersecurity teams from Asia, Europe and the United States — as well as multiple former and current Pinduoduo employees — after receiving a tipoff.

Multiple experts identified the presence of malware on the Pinduoduo app that exploited vulnerabilities in Android operating systems. Company insiders said the exploits were utilized to spy on users and competitors, allegedly to boost sales.

“We haven’t seen a mainstream app like this trying to escalate their privileges to gain access to things that they’re not supposed to gain access to,” said Mikko Hyppönen, chief research officer at WithSecure, a Finnish cybersecurity firm.

“This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.”

This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.

Malware, short for malicious software, refers to any software developed to steal data or interfere with computer systems and mobile devices.

Evidence of sophisticated malware in the Pinduoduo app comes amid intense scrutiny of Chinese-developed apps like TikTok over concerns about data security.

Some American lawmakers are pushing for a national ban on the popular short-video app, whose CEO Shou Chew was grilled by Congress for five hours last week about its relations with the Chinese government.

The revelations are also likely to draw more attention to Pinduoduo’s international sister app, Temu, which is topping US download charts and fast expanding in other Western markets. Both are owned by Nasdaq-listed PDD, a multinational company with roots in China.

While Temu has not been implicated, Pinduoduo’s alleged actions risk casting a shadow over its sister app’s global expansion.

There is no evidence that Pinduoduo has handed data to the Chinese government. But as Beijing enjoys significant leverage over businesses under its jurisdiction, there are concerns from US lawmakers that any company operating in China could be forced to cooperate with a broad range of security activities.

The findings follow Google’s suspension of Pinduoduo from its Play Store in March, citing malware identified in versions of the app.

An ensuing report from Bloomberg said a Russian cybersecurity firm had also identified potential malware in the app.

Pinduoduo has previously rejected “the speculation and accusation that Pinduoduo app is malicious.”

CNN has contacted PDD multiple times over email and phone for comment, but has not received a response.

Pinduoduo, which boasts a user base that accounts for three quarters of China’s online population and a market value three times that of eBay (EBAY), wasn’t always an online shopping behemoth.

Founded in 2015 in Shanghai by Colin Huang, a former Google employee, the startup was fighting to establish itself in a market long dominated by e-commerce stalwarts Alibaba (BABA) and JD.com (JD).

It succeeded by offering steep discounts on friends-and-family group buying orders and focusing on lower-income rural areas.

Pinduoduo posted triple digit growth in monthly users until the end of 2018, the year it listed in New York. By the middle of 2020, though, the increase in monthly users had slowed to around 50% and would continue to decline, according to its earnings reports.

It was in 2020, according to a current Pinduoduo employee, that the company set up a team of about 100 engineers and product managers to dig for vulnerabilities in Android phones, develop ways to exploit them — and turn that into profit.

According to the source, who requested anonymity for fear of reprisals, the company only targeted users in rural areas and smaller towns initially, while avoiding users in megacities such as Beijing and Shanghai.

“The goal was to reduce the risk of being exposed,” they said.

By collecting expansive data on user activities, the company was able to create a comprehensive portrait of users’ habits, interests and preferences, according to the source.

This allowed it to improve its machine learning model to offer more personalized push notifications and ads, attracting users to open the app and place orders, they said.

The team was disbanded in early March, the source added, after questions about their activities came to light.

PDD didn’t reply to CNN’s repeated requests for comment on the team.

Approached by CNN, researchers from Tel Aviv-based cyber firm Check Point Research, Delaware-based app security startup Oversecured and Hyppönen’s WithSecure conducted independent analysis of the 6.49.0 version of the app, released on Chinese app stores in late February.

Google Play is not available in China, and Android users in the country download their apps from local stores. In March, when Google suspended Pinduoduo, it said it had found malware in off-Play versions of the app.

The researchers found code designed to achieve “privilege escalation”: a type of cyberattack that exploits a vulnerable operating system to gain a higher level of access to data than it’s supposed to have, according to experts.

“Our team has reverse engineered that code and we can confirm that it tries to escalate rights, tries to gain access to things normal apps wouldn’t be able to do on Android phones,” said Hyppönen.

The app was able to continue running in the background and prevent itself from being uninstalled, which allowed it to boost its monthly active user rates, Hyppönen said. It also had the ability to spy on competitors by tracking activity on other shopping apps and getting information from them, he added.

Check Point Research additionally identified ways in which the app was able to evade scrutiny.

The app deployed a method that allowed it to push updates without an app store review process meant to detect malicious applications, the researchers said.

They also identified in some plug-ins the intent to obscure potentially malicious components by hiding them under legitimate file names, such as Google’s.

“Such a technique is widely used by malware developers that inject malicious code into applications that have legitimate functionality,” they said.

In China, about three quarters of smartphone users are on the Android system. Apple (AAPL)’s iPhone has 25% market share, according to Daniel Ives of Wedbush Securities.

Sergey Toshin, the founder of Oversecured, said Pinduoduo’s malware specifically targeted different Android-based operating systems, including those used by Samsung, Huawei, Xiaomi and Oppo.

CNN has reached out to these companies for comment.

Toshin described Pinduoduo as “the most dangerous malware” ever found among mainstream apps.

“I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive,” he said.

Most phone manufacturers globally customize the core Android software, the Android Open Source Project (AOSP), to add unique features and applications to their own devices.

Toshin found Pinduoduo to have exploited about 50 Android system vulnerabilities. Most of the exploits were tailor made for customized parts known as the original equipment manufacturer (OEM) code, which tends to be audited less often than AOSP and is therefore more prone to vulnerabilities, he said.

Pinduoduo also exploited a number of AOSP vulnerabilities, including one which was flagged by Toshin to Google in February 2022. Google fixed the bug this March, he said.

I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive.

According to Toshin, the exploits allowed Pinduoduo access to users’ locations, contacts, calendars, notifications and photo albums without their consent. They were also able to change system settings and access users’ social network accounts and chats, he said.

Of the six teams CNN spoke to for this story, three did not conduct full examinations. But their primary reviews showed that Pinduoduo asked for a large number of permissions beyond the normal functions of a shopping app.

They included “potentially invasive permissions” such as “set wallpaper” and “download without notification,” said René Mayrhofer, head of the Institute of Networks and Security at the Johannes Kepler University Linz in Austria.

Suspicions about malware in Pinduoduo’s app were first raised in late February in a report by a Chinese cybersecurity firm called Dark Navy. Even though the analysis didn’t directly name the shopping giant, the report spread quickly among other researchers, who did name the company. Some of the analysts followed up with their own reports confirming the original findings.

Soon after, on March 5, Pinduoduo issued a new update of its app, version 6.50.0, which removed the exploits, according to two experts who CNN spoke to.

Two days after the update, Pinduoduo disbanded the team of engineers and product managers who had developed the exploits, according to the Pinduoduo source.

The next day, team members found themselves locked out of Pinduoduo’s bespoke workplace communication app, Knock, and lost access to files on the company’s internal network. Engineers also found their access to big data, data sheets and the log system revoked, the source said.

Most of the team were transferred to work at Temu. They were assigned to different departments at the subsidiary, with some working on marketing or developing push notifications, according to the source.

A core group of about 20 cybersecurity engineers who specialize in finding and exploiting vulnerabilities remain at Pinduoduo, they said.

Toshin of Oversecured, who looked into the update, said although the exploits were removed, the underlying code was still there and could be reactivated to carry out attacks.

Pinduoduo has been able to grow its user base against a backdrop of the Chinese government’s regulatory clampdown on Big Tech that began in late 2020.

That year, the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology launched a sweeping crackdown on apps that illegally collect and use personal data.

The Personal Information Protection Law stipulates that no party should illegally collect, process or transmit personal information. They’re also banned from exploiting internet-related security vulnerabilities or engaging in actions that endanger cybersecurity.

Pinduoduo’s apparent malware would be a violation of those laws, tech policy experts say, and should have been detected by the regulator.

“This would be embarrassing for the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology, because this is their job,” said Kendra Schaefer, a tech policy expert at Trivium China, a consultancy. “They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.”

Pinduoduo did not appear on any of the lists.

They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.

CNN has reached out to the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology and the Cyberspace Administration of China for comment.

On Chinese social media, some cybersecurity experts questioned why regulators haven’t taken any action.

“Probably none of our regulators can understand coding and programming, nor do they understand technology. You can’t even understand the malicious code when it’s shoved right in front of your face,” a cybersecurity expert with 1.8 million followers wrote last week in a viral post on Weibo, a Twitter-like platform.

CNN’s Kristie Lu Stout and Sean Lyngaas contributed reporting.

#178. US prosecutors charge Iranian operative allegedly responsible for UK air force base attack

New indictment claims Mohammad Dawood al-Saadi, a dual Iranian and Iraqi national, plotted while in US custody

US federal prosecutors have said that an Iranian operative jailed in New York was responsible for the foiled attack on a UK air force base where American B-1 bombers were stationed.

A new indictment alleged that Mohammad Dawood al-Saadi, a dual Iranian and Iraqi national, plotted while in custody and then transmitted plans – using coded messages in calls to his family – to two co-conspirators to buy large quantities of chemicals that could be used as explosives in an attack on the RAF Fairford base in September.

Al-Saadi, who was accused of being associated with Kata’ib Hezbollah and Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps, was said to have planned the attack as retribution for the US war in Iran.

He had already been charged for his alleged links to 18 terrorist attacks in Europe and for allegedly planning to bomb a synagogue in New York as well, according to a statement by the southern district of New York.

He was already imprisoned when he began planning the attack, according to the indictment, which said he then transmitted his plans by using coded messages in calls to his family to pass on to Mohsen Mohajeri, an operative of the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps, and another Iranian, Mohammad Ali al-Hassani.

Al-Hassani then tasked others with surveilling the Fairfield RAF base and with procuring vans and barrels of fuel, and researching the purchase of chemical accelerants and metallic objects to be used as shrapnel, the indictment claimed.

Five men from London were arrested in the village of Whelford, near RAF Fairford, on 27 September on suspicion of explosives and terrorism offences, after a farmer reported vans blocking a road near the base while on her way back from a party.

RAF Fairford is the European home for US air force bombers and was used by the US during its bombing campaign against Iran. In July, Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) issued a statement noting RAF Fairford’s use by US bombers and saying any bases used by the US to attack Iran, including Fairford, were “legitimate targets”.

“As alleged, these defendants helped orchestrate a campaign of terror on behalf of terrorist organizations affiliated with Iran that targeted Jewish communities in Europe and the United States, and the British military base, RAF Fairford, housing American service members,” said the US attorney Jamie McDonald. “Even after one of their co-conspirators was arrested and placed in federal custody, the defendants allegedly continued coordinating terrorist activity through coded communications and a network of associates overseas.”

“The threat presented by Iran and Iran-backed terrorist groups to America and our allies is not hypothetical – it is very real,” he added.
