#147. Disgraced former leader Sheikh Hasina plans return to Bangladesh from exile

Former prime minister faces death penalty upon return after being sentenced in absentia over deaths in 2024 uprising

Sheikh Hasina is “confident” she will return to Bangladesh in December, according to exiled members of her party, after the disgraced former prime minister applied for a new passport this week.

Hasina has been living in self-imposed exile since August 2024 when she was toppled from power by a mass uprising against her authoritarian rule. She crossed the border to India, where she has since been allowed to live freely in Delhi.

Last November, a tribunal in Bangladesh found Hasina guilty of crimes against humanity and sentenced her to death over her crackdown on a student uprising that left an estimated 1,400 people dead.

Her return to Bangladesh is likely to be disruptive for the country where Hasina’s party, Awami League, is still banned and thousands of its members are in jail. Should she be allowed to return, she is likely to be arrested immediately.

Hasina first raised the prospect of going back to Bangladesh in August. Speaking to the Guardian this week, several exiled Awami League leaders also living in India confirmed that Hasina was serious and “confident” about returning by next month. They confirmed that several other party figures had already returned in anticipation.

On Monday, a British lawyer acting on behalf of Hasina wrote to the Bangladesh high commission in Delhi requesting a new passport and national identity card to be issued “in a timely fashion”, in a letter seen by the Guardian.

“The reason our leader Sheikh Hasina has applied for a passport is that she genuinely wants to return,” said a party leader based in the Indian city of Kolkata, who spoke on condition of anonymity because he is not authorised to speak to the media on the party’s behalf.

Hasina has rejected the charges against her and claimed the tribunal that convicted her was illegitimate. “She is confident about her return, which is why she has applied for a new passport and a national identity card. If the authorities do not issue her a passport, she will apply for a travel pass to make her return to Bangladesh possible,” said the Awami League leader.

Since Hasina’s conviction, the Bangladesh government has put pressure on the Indian authorities to extradite the former prime minister. However, the Indian government, which was close to Hasina while she was in power, has ignored the demands, straining ties between the two countries.

This week, the Bangladesh government said that, as a “terrorist”, Hasina would only be allowed to return via an “acceptable prisoner exchange” under the India-Bangladesh extradition treaty.

Aminul Islam, the chief prosecutor of the tribunal that sentenced Hasina, said there was “no legal scope” for her to be issued a new passport as she was a “convicted fugitive”.

Islam emphasised that Hasina would be hanged if she returned, as the deadline for her to appeal against her conviction had passed. “Sheikh Hasina has no option but to face the execution of the verdict,” he said.

Despite the abuses and oppression that mired her 15 years in power and subsequent conviction for crimes against humanity, Hasina still commands significant popularity among the thousands of Awami League cadre still in Bangladesh, many of whom have been driven underground.

Analysts said they believed her decision to return was driven by an attempt to revive her support base and destabilise the newly elected government, led by Tarique Rahman. It came to power in February during Bangladesh’s first democratic election in decades.

The government has since struggled with inflation and protests that shut down garment factories, which the Awami League hopes to use to its advantage.

Another Awami League leader said all those in exile intended to follow Hasina back to Bangladesh, despite many of them facing serious criminal charges and having been given lengthy prison sentences in absentia.

“Some of us have been sentenced in absentia by kangaroo courts, and we know we will be jailed. But we are ready to face the consequences and return to our own people. We will fight this politically,” said the senior leader, who is based in India.

Some senior party figures have returned in recent months, including the former MP Mustaque Ahmed Ruhi. He was immediately arrested on his return in September and faces prosecution over an alleged murder during the 2024 uprising.

Exiled Awami League leaders have continued to meet in Kolkata, where many of them are living, and are routinely summoned to Delhi to meet Hasina.

Speaking over email this week, Hasina told the Associated Press: “If death is my destiny, I would rather die on the soil of my own country, among my own people.”

#143. Iran was behind cyber-attack on French supermarket freezers, Paris says

French foreign minister vows European sanctions against Tehran over ‘unprecedented’ hacks last month

France has said Iran was responsible for a massive cyber-attack on supermarket freezers and chiller cabinets that was described by its foreign minister as being an act of “unprecedented scale and unprecedented aggression”.

Jean-Noël Barrot said “Europe-wide sanctions” would be imposed against those responsible, adding that the attacks – which took control of digital thermostats to push temperatures up to above 50C – constituted “a major violation of the nation’s interests”.

At a press briefing during the G7 summit in Paris, he said: “Thanks to the remarkable efforts of government agencies, I am able to confirm that the Islamic Republic of Iran’s Revolutionary Guards are responsible for this attack.”

He said the Iranian ambassador had been summoned “to protest in the strongest possible terms against this extremely serious act.”

Swathes of French supermarkets were hit in the cyber-attacks on 11 and 12 October. Some were forced to throw away entire inventories of perishable food.

Similar incidents have been reported since, including on supermarket fridges in other European countries last week and on a French blood bank.

The Paris public prosecutor, Laure Beccuau, said on Monday that specialist investigators and government agencies had identified “a modus operandi” characteristic of the Iranian group Shahid Kaveh, a cyber warfare unit linked to the Islamic Revolutionary Guards Corps (IRGC).

This included “the use of Farsi and IP addresses traced back to devices located in Iran”, she said.

Beccuau said her office had opened an investigation against the head of Shahid Kaveh, his deputies and “others who will be identified” on charges of “fraudulent attacks on an automated data processing system committed as part of an organised crime ring and of causing damage dangerous to persons”.

She said the offences were “aggravated by the fact they were committed to serve a foreign power – in this case Iran”.

Gérôme Billois, a partner at the cybersecurity and technology company Wavestone, said Shahid Kaveh had been active since 2020 and was known to have carried out cyber-attacks mostly on water distribution systems in the US.

“Two countries are known for this kind of attack: Iran and Russia. Given that recent cyber-attacks on supermarkets and the blood bank in France concerned freezers it is reasonable to think Iran is directly linked to both,” he said,

“For the moment they are attacking easy equipment but as a state group that has the means, the determination and the time, we should be prepared to see attacks on other systems like water or electricity.”

During the attack last month, hackers remotely overrode the temperature setting on thousands of freezers, fridges and chiller cabinets in as many as 700 supermarkets and shops across France, pushing them from as low as -27C (-16.6F) to well over 50C (122F) in some cases.

A digest of the morning's main headlines from the Europe edition emailed direct to you every week day

Thousands of tons of food had to be dumped and in some stores the extreme temperature rise sparked fires, triggering sprinklers and water damage.

Hackers last week accessed control systems at cold stores stocking blood products. The French blood service (EFS) said temperature and defrosting thresholds and been raised to “very high level” but that technicians had been alerted by alarm systems allowing them to disconnect the cold stores before the products were destroyed. It is not clear whether the two attacks are linked.

The following day retailers in 11 European countries including the UK, Belgium, Estonia, Ireland, Finland, Luxembourg, the Netherlands and Sweden reported problems with refrigeration systems.

A regional branch of France’s Crédit Mutuel bank also reported losing more than €100m in a cyber-attack on Friday. The bank said it had recuperated some of the money.

The European Commission did not immediately comment on the French foreign minister’s statement. Kaja Kallas, the head of foreign affairs for the EU said: “We have the foreign affairs council next Monday, so we will have the opportunity to discuss this. These cyber incidents occurring in Europe, if attributed [to Iran], also call for a strong response on our part.”

In a significant decision this year, the EU designated the IRGC a terrorist organisation, ending years of division over the issue. The designation became possible after France dropped its long-held opposition, having previously feared it would damage diplomatic channels and the interests of French nationals in Iran.

#141. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

French politician, Jean Luc Mélenchon who came third in the 2022 presidential elections criticized both Schiappa’s appearance and the decision by French President Emmanuel Macron to give an interview to children’s magazine, Pif Gadget, this week.

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.

#139. Witnesses recount struggle to save people buried in ‘horrifying’ Gaza landslide

High winds and heavy rain triggered collapse of sand bank overlooking camp for displaced families

Witnesses have described the frantic struggle to save three members of a family buried by a landslide triggered by high winds and heavy rain in Gaza.

A sand bank overlooking a camp for displaced families south of Deir al-Balah collapsed on Sunday, killing five people and crushing at least three tents.

“It was a horrifying sight,” said Abdul Karim al-Sahhar, 30, originally from Izbat Beit Hanoun in northern Gaza. “We saw people buried beneath the sand and rocks. None of them was moving or making a sound. Even the tent had disappeared completely. There was no trace of it left.”

Among the casualties were an eight-year-old, a six-year-old and their mother.

Sahhar, whose own shelter was badly damaged in the landslide, said the children’s father was outside the family tent and so survived. “One of his children had called him to come and eat with them,” he said. “He was a little late returning, and then the collapse happened.

“The father started shouting: ‘Save my children! Save my children!’ People came and started digging [but] there was a huge boulder on top of the victims. The young men had to break it into pieces so they could reach the people buried underneath.”

Abu Darbali, another witness, said the collapse of the embankment sounded like an explosion. “We found that three tents were covered completely after the mountain collapsed on top of them,” he told Reuters. “People were under the rubble and we couldn’t see them.”

Video posted to social media showed people using hammers to break large rocks and others removing sand with shovels, using flashlights to search for those trapped underneath.

The UN said more than 26,000 households had reported damage to shelters or belongings after the weekend’s rainstorms.

The vast majority of Gaza’s population of 2.3 million live in tent encampments or the ruins of bombed-out buildings, ‌with reconstruction yet to begin after Israel’s relentless two-year military assault launched after the Hamas attacks on southern Israel in October 2023.

“The collapse [this weekend] was waiting to happen. [Gaza] is extremely overcrowded. People are just trying to find a place wherever,” said Toby Fricker, a spokesperson for Unicef, which has been working to prevent similar tragedies and improve drainage in camps for displaced people.

In a statement, the Hamas-run government media office said about “70,000 tents sheltering tens of thousands of displaced people were flooded on the first day” of rains. It also noted the “collapse of dozens of cracked walls and roofs, which caused injuries”, some critical, in various areas of the tiny territory.

A US-brokered ceasefire last year called for the eventual reconstruction of Gaza, but little progress has been made. Negotiators have failed to agree on terms for Hamas to disarm or for Israel to withdraw its troops, which control about two-thirds of the territory ‌from which residents have been ordered to leave.

Israel says no reconstruction can happen before Hamas is disarmed.

Sahhar said he and his family were now homeless. “My tent and everything I own are now buried under the sand. My mattress, household utensils and all my belongings – everything was buried. I could not retrieve anything,” he said.

Mustafa Qazzaat, a member of the Gaza City council, said Israel had not allowed prefabricated homes to shelter the displaced since the truce was announced, and only permitted the entry of limited numbers of tents.

Many so-called “dual use” items remain restricted, such as metal tent poles. Humanitarian workers said they instead had used wooden poles fashioned from palettes used to transport food.

Cogat, the Israeli defence ministry body that oversees aid to Gaza, said more than 91,000 tents and over 8,200 trucks containing tarpaulins, blankets and winter clothing had reached Gaza, as well as convoys carrying supplies of fuel and engine oil to power pumping facilities and other humanitarian installations.

Earlier this year, an assessment compiled by shelter specialists in Gaza revealed that thousands of tents supplied by China, Egypt and Saudi Arabia to shelter displaced Palestinians offered only limited protection against rain and wind.

#134. Trump says US war in Iran ‘effectively over’ as he plays down escalation concerns

President, after talks with Qatari PM, suggests end to war coming ‘fairly soon’ and military escalation not needed

Donald Trump has said that the war in Iran is “effectively over” as he played down concerns that the US would escalate its military campaign against Tehran after the midterm elections.

During a White House press conference, Trump said that Israel and Gulf allies in the region were well protected from Iran and that an escalation was not needed.

“At this point, taking the military action doesn’t seem to be a very necessary thing – but we’re locked and loaded, ready to go for any reason,” he told reporters during a briefing at the White House.

The remarks followed talks in the Oval Office with the Qatari prime minister, Sheikh Mohammed bin Abdulrahman Al Thani, who has lobbied Trump alongside other Gulf countries not to resume the fighting against Iran.

“We’re seeing momentum toward the end of this conflict. I think it’s going to be ending fairly soon,” he added.

Trump has frequently exaggerated claims about the status of negotiations with Iran, saying a final peace deal was imminent dozens of times during the nearly nine-month long war.

The US had continued to move troops to the Middle East in the run-up to the midterm elections. There was speculation that the Trump administration was waiting for the elections to take place before resuming a military campaign, in order to prevent a backlash at the polls.

Republicans were nevertheless routed in a historic defeat in which they lost both houses of Congress, with the vice-president, JD Vance, declaring soon after that the Iran war was a key reason for the defeats.

Trump earlier on Monday said on Truth Social that the leaders of Qatar, Saudi Arabia, UAE, Bahrain, and Kuwait would prefer the “status quo, in other words, a continuation of the Greatest Naval Blockade in History”.

He repeated a mantra that Iran would not be allowed to develop a nuclear weapon, and added that if the situation changed then the US was ready to resume full-scale military action against Iran.

“At this point, however, that seems to be quite unlikely,” he continued. “Israel, and the entire Middle East, are protected from Iranian danger and threats. We are seeing positive momentum toward an end to this Conflict, and will ensure that America’s interests are at all times protected. At the conclusion of this Military Engagement, the American People, and the World, will be far safer and more secure.”

#133. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#130. Qantas to outsource 850 local jobs with most roles moving to India

Qantas CEO Vanessa Hudson apologised for the "hard news" of 850 job cuts

Qantas will outsource 850 local corporate jobs to consulting giant Accenture with most roles moving to India as the airline fast-tracks AI investment.

In an internal email to staff on Tuesday, the Australian company's CEO Vanessa Hudson said "this is hard news for some and I'm sorry for the uncertainty it brings".

The job cuts will begin mid-2027, she said, and start with finance, procurement, people and marketing roles shifting "mostly" to teams in India and "some in Australia" as the firm seeks to modernise its IT systems.

Hudson said the "scale and speed of change in technology and AI is something none of us has seen in our lifetime", and it was in talks with unions and staff on the plans.

The move comes days after the airline provided a market update, flagging fare increases and flight cuts amid higher fuel costs.

On its new multi-year deal with Accenture, Hudson said it was about "modernising" the airline's IT systems to become "more efficient".

Qantas spends more than AU$1bn (US$691m; Â£514m) a year on technology, she said, and the company's back-end systems needed to be upgraded.

"Like every airline, we run on technology built up over decades for a different era, including more than 1,000 legacy applications that were never designed to work together," she said.

The deal with Accenture means "we will redesign and simplify parts of how our corporate functions work and reduce duplication", Hudson said, so staff can "spend more time on work that creates value for our customers and the business".

The company said that "in time", the work that Accenture takes over will be "transformed further with automation and AI".

Staff affected by the job cuts will be redeployed "where possible", the firm added, including new roles in Australia.

Qantas - which employs about 28,000 staff in Australia and another 4,000 globally - said thousands of its staff already use AI in their daily work.

The BBC has contacted the Australian Services Union for comment.

#128. New Knesset speaker could be key to ousting Netanyahu as Israel’s PM

Opposition politicians get first chance to take control of levers of power, even as they struggle to form government

Israel’s new Knesset will be sworn in on Tuesday, giving opposition politicians their first chance to take control of some levers of power, even as they struggle to form a new government to unseat Benjamin Netanyahu.

High on the agenda will be replacing the speaker, who wields immense influence by controlling the legislature’s agenda and committee chairs. The current speaker is Amir Ohana, a close ally of Netanyahu.

He will be replaced by Matan Kahana, from the Yashar party led by Gadi Eisenkot, the former Israeli military chief who got the presidential mandate to try to form a coalition government at the weekend.

Kahana, a former minister, fighter pilot and special forces officer, has the explicit backing of all four Jewish Israeli parties in Eisenkot’s “change” bloc. The two other Palestinian-led opposition parties have said replacing Ohana is a priority so are expected to give Kahana their votes.

The opposition parties hold 67 seats in the 120-seat legislature, enough to install a consensus choice for speaker. They will request a vote on Tuesday afternoon, and Ohana then has up to a week to bring it to the floor.

Avigdor Lieberman, who heads another opposition party, is nominated to head the foreign affairs and defence committee. It will play a central role in drawing up planned legislation covering the conscription of ultra-Orthodox men, a key campaign issue for Lieberman.

The effort to wrest away control of the Knesset is significant enough that more than 160 former top security officials, including heads of the military, intelligence and police, warned at the weekend that rightwing activists were planning to disrupt the day with violent attacks.

“Actors who oppose replacing the Knesset speaker intend to go to the Knesset on the day of the swearing-in ceremony and to prevent the MKs and their family members from entering the building,” a forum of retired commanders said in a letter to serving security chiefs.

“These same actors are encouraging tens of thousands of citizens who support their stance to come and to help them with their mission,” Ma’ariv newspaper quoted the letter as saying.

All opposition parties have described Netanyahu as a threat to the state and are keen to start moving against him in the Knesset, despite divides blocking the formation of a coalition.

Several of the Jewish-Israeli opposition parties in Eisenkot’s bloc have sworn not to sit with the Palestinian-led parties, which hold more than a quarter of the bloc’s seats, or even join a minority government that has their backing.

Eisenkot has vowed to broker a national emergency government, promising to achieve it with “creative and unconventional political acts”.

In a sign of challenges ahead, he effectively warned that some election promises would have to be broken to unseat Netanyahu. “No one will be able to fulfil all the [campaign] promises they made, and all of us will need to compromise. That is also leadership, to know when to decide and set priorities,” he said.

There have been protests across Israel urging Jewish-Israeli opposition parties to put unseating Netanyahu ahead of a racist commitment to excluding Palestinian-led parties from government.

Palestinian citizens of Israel make up about 20% of the population and voted in unprecedented numbers this year, giving the parties 19 seats. Without them, the opposition bloc would have fewer seats than Netanyahu’s diminished coalition.

Lieberman has been trying to seek an alternative path to government through an alliance with ultra-Orthodox parties.

They have long been Netanyahu allies but have been frustrated by his failure to deliver on the promise to pass broad military draft exemptions for their community into law. Lieberman has campaigned hard for universal conscription, but appears to prefer compromise on this issue to working with Palestinians.

“Has the End of Times arrived?” the leading Haredi (ultra-Orthodox) journalist Arye Erlich wrote in a column for Yedioth Ahronoth newspaper.

“Has Avigdor Lieberman … the biggest persecutor of Haredim Israeli politics has seen in decades, suddenly come to believe that he can compromise on a military draft bill and sit in a coalition government alongside the Haredim?”

Even if Eisenkot does broker a coalition deal, he may delay announcing it until Netanyahu’s speaker has been replaced. Netanyahu is a master of political survival and last-minute deals, fracturing past coalitions by luring defectors into his own alliance.

Ohana could delay signing in a new government for up to a week, and some of the opposition do not want to give Netanyahu that much time to attack the pressure points of any fragile deal.

If Eisenkot cannot form a government, the mandate is passed to other leaders, and if they fail, Israel must hold elections again. In the meantime Netanyahu and his far-right allies would stay on as a caretaker government.

Although Netanyahu is calling for his opponents to join him in a unity government, a long period of limbo followed by a new vote offers him a likelier path back to power.

If an alternative government took power, it would be harder for Netanyahu to destabilise it, said Gideon Rahat, a chair in political science at the Hebrew University of Jerusalem.

“Once they establish a government, they might be a minority government, it might be hard for them to do things, but nevertheless, they will be governing and it would be hard to oust them.”

#126. Australia considers criminal penalties for rogue AI in wake of OpenAI hack

PM Anthony Albanese says the law needs to catch up with a "fast-changing world"

Australia plans to create a new AI safety regulator and has flagged criminal penalties for tech companies who do not comply, months after an OpenAI hack.

In September, Australia's Prime Minister Anthony Albanese revealed that a rogue OpenAI agent had "infiltrated" a government health website and other systems, and criticised the company for taking months to tell them about the hack.

On Tuesday, he released the 18 recommendations made after a "rapid review" into the breaches including new safety and security standards for AI companies such as mandatory reporting of hacks.

AI brings opportunities, he said, but Australia must prepare for the "new risks" from this "rapidly advancing technology".

"The law needs to get ahead of and, in some cases, make sure that it catches up with, what is a very fast changing world," Albanese said.

One of the recommendations suggests changes to criminal law to have a standalone "failure to prevent" offence .

This would criminalise instances where a company develops "an AI model that engages in harmful conduct" and "fails to take reasonable steps to prevent that harm".

The review also recommended "mandatory, time-bound AI safety reporting" of hacks.

This point addresses what Albanese had earlier called his "disappointment" in the months-long delay for OpenAI to tell his government about the breach.

In June, a statistics portal containing "non-sensitive" data from Australia's universal healthcare scheme Medicare was hacked by a rogue OpenAI agent.

OpenAI said it only learnt of the breach in August while reviewing "misaligned model activity" and emailed a general inbox of an Australian government agency on 10 September.

It took several more days before the email was escalated to Australia's cybersecurity centre, with a message then sent to a government minister ahead of the prime minister being told.

Albanese said he spoke to OpenAI boss Sam Altman and raised "Australia's extreme concern about this incident", with Altman acknowledging there were "issues with protocols" at OpenAI.

Days after the hacks were revealed, OpenAI delayed its rollout of a new AI model, citing safety concerns.

#121. Israeli opposition hurries to seize control as new Knesset is sworn in

Former military chief Gadi Eisenkot has mandate to try to form a government – with Netanyahu waiting if he fails

Opposition lawmakers have wasted no time after Israel’s new Knesset was sworn in, deploying their majority to seize control of the legislature from Benjamin Netanyhau and his allies.

Although they are still too divided to form a government, the opposition requested a vote for a new speaker, who sets the Knesset’s agenda. Once Netanyahu loyalist Amir Ohana has been replaced, the six opposition parties will be able to work on key legislation even if they broker a coalition.

Priorities include setting up a state commission of inquiry into the 7 October 2023 attacks and introducing term limits for Israel’s prime ministers, which could block Netanyahu from taking power again. He has ruled Israel for 19 of the last 30 years.

Gadi Eisenkot, a former Israeli military chief, now has the presidential mandate to try to form a government. If he fails to broker a deal within six weeks, a second candidate – probably Netanyahu – will get a second chance. Further failure is likely to lead to another election.

Netanyahu responded to the swearing in of the new Knesset on Tuesday with a racist attack on two Palestinian-led opposition parties, whose voters were essential to unseating his coalition, calling their lawmakers “terror supporters”.

Eisenkot denounced his speech as a “delusional, divisive, hate-filled and shameful” exploitation of a state occasion, that showed the prime minister was unfit for office.

Yair Golan, leader of the centre-left Democrats, and Yoav Segalovich, a Jewish-Israeli former police chief who ran on the slate of Islamist Ra’am party, both walked out of Netanyahu’s speech in protest.

Palestinian citizens of Israel make up about 20% of the country’s population. Netanyahu put attacks on these voters and their parties at the heart of his campaign, and since polling day has promoted groundless accusations of fraud in their communities in a bid to undermine results.

Election officials say there was no evidence of irregularities that affected the election outcome or compromised its integrity.

A period of prolonged, difficult coalition negotiations or even a new round of elections would extend Netanyahu’s hold on power as caretaker prime minister. But if the fractured opposition can make progress on key legislation, his grip on the state would be weakened.

Eisenkot’s Yashar party submitted a series of bills to the Knesset moments after the swearing in, with backing from other opposition parties.

In addition to term limits and the 7 October inquiry, legislation includes several bills designed to revoke the “judicial overhaul” pursued by Netanyahu, which weakened the power of Israel’s judiciary and the attorney general.

Ohana has tabled the vote on a new speaker to replace him for next Tuesday, the latest date possible. The opposition have also nominated candidates to take over key defence and finance committees.

Eisenkot has admitted the path to power is “extraordinarily complex”, because of incompatible election promises made by different parties, but described ousting Netanyahu as a national emergency that could require breaking promises.

At the swearing-in ceremony, the president, Isaac Herzog, warned lawmakers they should do everything in their power to avoid a second election.

“Reach out a hand for compromise, even between ⁠rivals … Look upon one another with generosity. There is much work to be done here, and it requires the ability and experience found in every part of this House.“

#119. Oxford museum returns ancestral Naga skulls to India

A delegation of 30 people had travelled to Oxford from India's Nagaland

The University of Oxford has returned the remains of 39 people taken from the Naga community during British colonial rule in India.

A delegation of around 30 people from Nagaland, a mountainous state in north-eastern India, attended a ceremony at Oxford's Pitt Rivers Museum on Monday to mark the handover.

The remains, most of which are skulls, will be taken back to India and given what Naga tribal leaders have described as a "respectful rest". There are plans to bury them under a joint memorial in a park in Nagaland.

Museum director Laura Van Broekhoven described it as "a historic moment that will be part of stories told to next generations".

She said the remains were finally on their way home after overcoming "so much red tape everywhere".

The human remains were displayed at the Oxford museum for decades before being removed from public view in 2020 as part of what it described as a "decolonisation process".

The museum formally handed over the remains and signed an agreement with Naga leaders on Monday

The Pitt Rivers Museum holds more than 6,000 objects associated with Naga culture, believed to be the largest such collection in the world. Most were acquired during British colonial rule in India and Myanmar (the former Burma).

The museum's website lists 219 items as human remains.

These were added to the museum's collection between 1910 and 1940 and mostly collected by two colonial administrators who were anthropologists.

The museum's website, external mentions that the objects collected during this period were "frequently confiscated during punitive military expeditions, rather than freely given".

During their visit to Oxford, the Naga delegation also met descendants of one of the anthropologists involved in collecting the remains.

"The manner in which these human remains were taken away from our land leaves much for us to speculate," one of the tribal leaders, Thejao Vihienuo, told AFP.

"I suspect that nobody would have given the skull of his father or his grandfather as a gift to another person."

Anthropologists say that the Naga people once practised head hunting, severing the heads of enemies as trophies. But they also kept the heads of their own chiefs at the centre of villages.

The Pitt Rivers Museum, founded in the 19th Century, has previously returned human remains to indigenous communities in Australia, New Zealand and the US.

The latest handover to India concerns only the 39 ancestral remains and comes after a visit by a Naga delegation to Oxford in 2025, when it formally requested their return.

The museum had announced in September that the remains would be returned to India.

#117. US officials seek ‘final end’ to Russia-Ukraine war, as Abu Dhabi talks set to continue

First substantive peace talks since February include US proposal for ‘long-term security architecture’ between Moscow and Europe

US officials have said they are seeking a “final end” to the war between Russia and Ukraine as trilateral talks got under way in Abu Dhabi, the first substantive negotiations between the two countries since February.

The talks, which are set to go into a planned second day on Wednesday, are also the first since the US launched joint strikes with Israel against Iran, and they follow bruising midterm elections that have weakened Donald Trump at home.

The Ukrainian president, Volodymyr Zelenskyy, who like Vladimir Putin was not in Abu Dhabi, described the discussions on the first day as “constructive and fairly substantive”.

“We discussed in detail the substance of security guarantees for Ukraine and the foundations for their long-term implementation,” he said in a social media post.

Part of the Trump administration’s proposal involves drawing up a new “long-term security architecture” between Russia and Europe, administration officials have said.

European officials are concerned that Russia may stick to its hardline demands that Nato should roll back its military footprint across eastern Europe, which would threaten to leave allies vulnerable to Moscow’s influence at a time of historic tensions with the west.

European officials held separate discussions on Tuesday with representatives from Ukraine and the US, where they agreed “any future arrangements must respect sovereignty, territorial integrity, independent decision-making, and provide credible security guarantees for Ukraine”, according to a joint statement.

European leaders are not convinced Russia intends to negotiate in good faith and want to be involved in discussions, although there are signs of unease about the proliferating strands of peace talks. Italy’s prime minister, Giorgia Meloni, last week complained about the “disorderly” process of meetings and phone calls that, she argued, weakened Europe’s ability to speak with one voice.

The US secretary of state, Marco Rubio, said on Tuesday it was a goal at some point to expand the Russia-US-Ukraine talks to include other European voices.

“There’s always this concern in Europe that they’re going to be left out, that America’s going to cut some deal with Russia and leave Europe out of the equation. Well, Europe’s at the table. They’re with us,” Rubio told reporters during a visit to Paris, speaking after the UAE talks had finished for the day.

Since his first decade in power, Putin has complained about Nato’s eastward expansion and sought to renegotiate the balance of power between Nato and Russia – but never before have those demands been embraced by a sitting US administration, which has made it clear that it is desperate to get to a deal.

Previewing the discussions, a US administration official said the US would “need Europe at the table” to end the war. He said negotiators could also revive the Nato-Russian council that was suspended after Russia’s full-scale invasion in 2022, and potentially lift sanctions against Russia to get to a deal.

The US officials also claimed that they had discussed with Russia potential security guarantees to Ukraine similar to Nato’s article 5, which protects member states in case of an attack. Russia has strongly opposed Nato admitting Ukraine or western countries placing troops in Ukraine.

“What they would be getting would be effectively very similar to Nato,” an administration official said.

“Getting a US security guarantee is a really, really big deal,” the official added, saying it would be up to Donald Trump and Ukraine’s Zelenskyy to discuss. “And if he wants to do that, that’s a very, very big deal in terms of resources and funding and military support.”

Critics have expressed scepticism about what may trigger those security guarantees, however, noting that article 5 does not oblige Nato member states to defend others, and that cyber-attacks and other forms of hybrid warfare have blurred the line for what would require a military response.

Jared Kushner, Trump’s son-in-law and a lead negotiator for the US, described a potential new framework for Europe in general terms on Monday, saying the US would seek “stronger consultation, reliable crisis communications, greater military transparency, and reciprocal measures to reduce escalation risks, while fully respecting the sovereignty and independent decision-making of every nation”.

A European security official said that the US proposal was not yet clear and that they were bracing themselves for the US to pose serious concessions to Russia during the Abu Dhabi consultations.

The EU high representative for foreign policy, Kaja Kallas, told a G7 foreign ministers meeting on Monday: “We already have a European security architecture in place. This includes international agreements under which Russia has undertaken obligations, including with regard to sovereignty and territorial integrity. The problem is that Russia is not respecting these agreements.”

Trump has also said that Ukraine would probably be expected to give up territory in a final deal, and would end with a “little bit different shape”.

After meeting the Kremlin adviser Kirill Dmitriev last week, Trump said he would seek a “big, beautiful agreement” that “might involve all of Europe”. Critics are concerned that Trump and Putin could try to go over the heads of European leaders in order to negotiate a peace that gives serious concessions to Russia.

“There’s a lot of baggage that people in Europe have about this idea,” said a US administration official, pointing specifically to Russia’s 2021 proposal that was viewed as an ultimatum to Nato to get out of eastern Europe.

At that time, Russia issued a list of demands to Nato that included a ban on expansion to new countries including Ukraine, a rollback of troops to its pre-1997 borders, and limits on where it could place missiles. Nato rejected that proposal, and Russia invaded Ukraine less than a month later.

The US administration official said: “We have to build something new that works today … Because if all the old architecture was working well, then we wouldn’t be in this long war. And so obviously something’s not working, and we need to come up with a way that when we end this war, it really is a final end to the war.”

#116. Trump says more US attacks on Iran 'highly unlikely'

Trump: "Iran militarily is effectively over"

Donald Trump has said "Iran militarily is effectively over", and said that there is "positive momentum" behind negotiations to end the Middle East conflict.

"We're just keeping it the status quo," the US president said on Monday. "And we're doing that really at the behest of countries that are their neighbours."

Trump's comments came after he met the Qatari prime minister. Qatar is a US ally in the Gulf region, and one of Iran's neighbours, which has been a key mediator in efforts to end the war.

Addressing reporters in the White House press briefing room, Trump said that renewed US attacks on Iran were "highly unlikely" but that the US was "locked and loaded" should the circumstances change.

Iranian officials have repeatedly expressed doubts about Trump's prior comments about resolving the conflict.

This is not the first time that Trump has said the Iran war is "militarily" over, but the circumstances for him are different now.

The president has hinted at de-escalation in Iran after a bruising set of midterm election results for the Republicans last week, which senior figures including Vice-President JD Vance blamed on the Iran war.

Ahead of the midterms, Trump had said US forces conducted what he described as "minor strikes" on an Iranian island in the Strait of Hormuz. However, in an interview with 60 Minutes that aired on Sunday, the president said that he did not currently see "any reason" to escalate the conflict.

Responding to a question over why he would not officially declare an end to the war, Trump said that some of the Gulf allies could still face Iranian aggression.

"We have some great allies there and we have to make sure they're not hurt," he said, suggesting that the US naval blockade on Iranian ports was helping to make the region more secure.

Global oil prices, which have fallen sharply since the midterms, dipped further after Trump's comments.

On Tuesday afternoon in Asia, Brent crude was more than 1% lower at $91.50 (Â£52) while US-traded was down by 1.2% at $85.82.

Could Democrats in Congress end the war in Iran?

Trump hosted Qatari Prime Minister Sheikh Mohammed bin Abdul Rahman Al Thani in the Oval Office earlier on Monday, saying they had an "excellent conversation" about negotiations to end the war.

In a post on X, the Qatari leader said he held "positive discussions" with Trump and Vance on "reaching a deal and bringing peace to the region".

Efforts to broker a peace between Washington and Tehran have largely been held indirectly through mediators since the war first broke out following joint US-Israeli strikes in February.

Saudi Arabia, Qatar and the United Arab Emirates are among the US's Gulf allies that have been hit by Iranian strikes over the course of the war, in addition to the threat of strikes that have led to major air travel disruptions in the region.

Republican congressman Thomas Massie plans to force a vote in the House of Representatives this week to impeach Defence Secretary Pete Hegseth

Post-election recriminations over the US war in Iran are already placing pressure on members of the Trump administration.

Republican congressman Thomas Massie launched a strong attack against Trump's Defence Secretary Pete Hegseth on Monday over the war in Iran, requesting a vote in the House of Representatives to impeach him.

Trump has backed Hegseth, who says he is "not worried" about the looming impeachment threats, calling Massie's effort a "sham".

While the move is unlikely to succeed with Republicans still in control of Congress until January, Democrats have expressed willingness to impeach Hegseth once the new session begins and they have control of both the House and the Senate.

#114. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”

Denmark announced a donation of 4.8bn kroner ($720m; £537m) in military aid, but did not disclose which weapons or equipment would be included or when they would be delivered.

Amid relentless Russian attacks, Zelenskyy has been urging allies to accelerate deliveries of Patriot interceptor missiles – by far the most effective weapon available to Ukraine for downing Russia’s deadly ballistic missiles.

Europe’s manufacturers could give up on attempting to compete with their Chinese counterparts by the end of the decade if current trends continue amid fears over mass job losses, the head of the European Union Chamber of Commerce in China has warned.

In a case of “if you can’t beat them, join them”, Jens Eskelund said European companies operating in China were now thinking “Made in China for China” could be replaced by a philosophy of “Made in China for the world”.

Eskelund, who heads the influential Beijing-headquartered body, said European manufacturers could find themselves at an existential crossroads by the end of the decade, asking themselves: “What is the point of trading?”

As the EU gets tough with China with a deficit running at a record £1bn (€1.18bn) a day and an increasing mood for protectionism in Brussels, Berlin and Paris, he said European countries might need to shift their policies to allow domestic manufacturers to become part of Chinese firms’ supply chains, rather than treat them as competitors.

Speaking after Russia, Ukraine and the US held trilateral talks today in the UAE, US secretary of state Marco Rubio has said the talks were “very productive”, but that there was still a lot of work to be done.

Reuters reports Rubio also said that it was a goal at some point to expand the trilateral talks to include other European voices.

Iran has responded to the accusation by France that it was responsible for the recent cyber-attacks on supermarket fridges in the European country, declaring them “baseless”.

AFP reports that in a statement, Tehran said “Iran’s foreign ministry categorically rejects the French government’s baseless and one-sided accusations against Iran regarding alleged cyber activities in France”, adding that it “strongly condemns this hackneyed policy of levelling accusations against the Islamic republic”.

The first day of talks between Russia, Ukraine and the US in the UAE have ended after four hours.

The press office of the Foreign Intelligence Service of Ukraine has stated they are expected to resume tomorrow. The Ukrainian delegation has also spoken to representatives from Nato and Europe as part of their mission.

We reported in an earlier post that ten people had been killed after dozens of vehicles were involved in a traffic accident on the Trakia motorway in south-eastern Bulgaria.

In an update, Bulgaria’s health minister, Katya Ivkova, said 10 people died at the scene and two others died while being transported to hospital, bringing the total deaths to 12. She said 46 other people were transported to medical facilities for treatment.

The EU’s climate chief, Wopke Hoekstra, has admitted that expectations were “low” from the Cop31 summit in Turkey this year, but said there was hope for “meaningful” progress on clean electricity.

#109. Israeli forces kill Palestinian boy in West Bank, health ministry says

Israeli forces carried out a raid in the Old City of Nablus, near Burqa, on Sunday

Israeli forces have shot dead a 14-year-old Palestinian boy during an operation in the occupied West Bank, Palestinian officials say.

Moataz Daghlas was killed on Monday evening in the village of Burqa, north-west of Nablus, according to the Palestinian health ministry.

The Israeli military said soldiers identified "several terrorists who intended to hurl stones" in an area where stones had previously been thrown at Israeli vehicles. The soldiers fired towards them "to remove the threat", it added.

The Palestinian Red Crescent said its teams treated two people with gunshot wounds, but that Israeli forces blocked their access to a third wounded person and detained three volunteer paramedics.

Moataz Daghlas's relatives said Israeli authorities were withholding his body.

Meanwhile, Palestinian news agency Wafa identified the two Palestinians wounded during the raid as a 17-year-old child, who it said was shot in the abdomen and pelvis, and a 19-year-old man, who was shot in the hand.

According to the UN, at least 98 Palestinians, including 22 children, have been killed by Israeli forces or settlers in the West Bank since the start of the year.

Six Israelis, both security forces and civilians, have been killed by Palestinians in the West Bank over the same period, it says.

The rules of engagement for Israeli forces were reportedly broadened after the deadly Hamas-led attacks on Israel on 7 October 2023, allowing soldiers increased discretion to use lethal force against a perceived threat.

In June, the UN Human Rights Office in the occupied Palestinian territory said it had verified the killing of 241 Palestinian children by Israeli security forces and settlers in the West Bank since then.

Most of the children were teenage boys and, in many cases, Israeli authorities accused them of having engaged in stone-throwing as justification, it found.

The UN office said its assessment was that none of the children killed while involved in confrontations with Israeli forces had posed an imminent threat to life, which it said was the only legal justification for the use of lethal force in the West Bank under international law.

#108. Russian naval aggression prompts new defensive initiative with ‘beer-drinking nations’

New alliance with the Netherlands and Baltic and Nordic countries comes in response to 41% increase in Russian naval activity around UK

Russian naval activity around the UK has increased by 41% in the first nine months of the year, a British defence minister has said, as the Royal Navy announced plans to step up a 10-country maritime defensive effort in response.

Luke Pollard, the minister for defence readiness, described the figure as “a huge increase”, justifying a new drone and warship initiative from “the alliance of beer-drinking nations” – the UK, the Netherlands and the Baltic and Nordic countries.

The plan is for sea drones from the UK and other countries to be able to work interchangeably with warships from countries across the Northern Navies Initiative at a time when the number of British frigates is falling.

“The model that we want is where British autonomy [drones] can be teamed with a British warship or a Danish warship or a Norwegian warship and can hand that autonomy off to an allied nation’s warship,” Pollard said.

A future Royal Navy would combine warships and drones “because that would increase our lethality”, Pollard said, sidestepping a debate about the number of UK frigates – which could drop from 12 in 2021 to three by 2030.

Last month, the cross-party defence select committee warned that the navy risked failing to deter Russian aggression in the seas around the UK because of shortfalls in the number and availability of warships and submarines.

Pollard blamed the Conservatives for capacity shortfalls, accusing them of cutting budgets and delaying investment when they were in government. “Now, because of the decisions taken 10, 15 years ago, we have fewer ships and fewer submarines than I would like available,” the minister said.

An extra £10bn a year allocated to defence in 2030 by the chancellor, John Healey, in October’s budget means that the UK could “send a clear demand signal to industry”, the minister said, to help tackle the Russian threat.

However, since the budget announcement, there have been no major defence spending announcements, with the UK sticking to the defence investment plan announced in June and fully funded in the budget. That included a £1.5bn investment in sea drones over four years.

Russian spy ships, such as the Yantar, have been sailing near UK waters this year trying to obtain “an increased picture of our critical national infrastructure”, Pollard said, as well as “mapping our undersea cables”.

Its frigates have also been active in the North Sea and the Channel throughout 2026, often escorting oil tankers and other trade vessels, and at one point firing shots near a pleasure yacht south of the Isle of Wight in June.

Oil tankers linked to Russia remained active, though were now often “sailing around the UK” to avoid coming through the Channel, Pollard said, after the seizure of one Russia-linked tanker, the Smyrtos, in the same month as the shooting incident.

The minister insisted that, if necessary, the Royal Navy was “ready to fight tonight”, even though it took three weeks for the UK to send a destroyer to Cyprus in the spring after the UK base at Akrotiri was struck by a drone fired by an Iranian proxy.

Russia’s northern fleet, based in the Barents Sea, retains “a really formidable undersea presence”, Pollard said, adding that Moscow had continued to invest in it throughout the Ukraine war as a means of threatening the UK.

The minister was speaking at the navy’s International Sea Power conference held on the Prince of Wales aircraft carrier in Oslo. Sailing the warship to Norway was “a really important deterrent signal that we stand ready to defend every single inch of Nato territory”, he said.

A further £90m will be spent on underwater drones and sensors to monitor the Greenland-Iceland-UK gap for Russian submarine activity, considered to be a strategic choke point in what the navy describes as the “high north”.

Ukraine has used cheap sea drones to destroy about a third of Russia’s Black Sea fleet, forcing it to relocate from Sevastopol in occupied Crimea to Novorossiysk, on the eastern Black Sea.

The former head of the country’s military, Valerii Zaluzhnyi, now Ukraine’s ambassador to the UK, said the increasingly complex use of sea drones meant that “it is likely that the world’s navies are on the verge of the most radical transformation since the rise of torpedo technology”.

#105. Britain willing to discuss paying into EU to join electricity market, says minister

Hamish Falconer says UK has ‘not accepted principle’ of linking electricity trade to ‘cohesion fund’ but two sides will discuss issue

Britain is willing to discuss paying towards infrastructure schemes in poorer parts of the EU in return for access to the European electricity market, according to the minister in charge of renegotiating the bilateral relationship.

Hamish Falconer, the cabinet minister in charge of EU talks, told the Guardian he was sure the two sides would discuss the issue after this month’s summit, as ministers look to deepen ties with the union over the next few years.

Andy Burnham, the prime minister, has said he wants a much closer relationship with the EU in the future, even raising the possibility of rejoining as a full member. But while senior figures in Brussels have said they are keen to discuss such an idea, they also want to see the UK begin to start paying for access to the European schemes it says it wants.

This weekend five European leaders welcomed the UK’s desire to join the European electricity market, but said Britain would have to pay into a so-called “cohesion fund” in order to do so.

While UK officials say they do not accept the link between the electricity reforms and cohesion fund, the minister admitted the UK would enter into talks about additional contributions.

“We have not accepted that principle,” Falconer said. “I understand that that’s what the European Union have asked for, but the UK government hasn’t accepted. Again, this is something no doubt we will discuss.”

Falconer has said he hopes next week’s keenly anticipated EU-UK summit “will go some real distance in undoing some of the harm that Brexit has done”. Negotiators hope to sign deals on food and drink, carbon emissions and youth mobility.

Any move to bring Britain into the EU electricity market, however, is likely to prove controversial. The EU cohesion fund promotes economic development in poorer regions in the bloc and UK officials will be cautious about any deal in which British taxpayers’ money could be spent on infrastructure projects in central and eastern Europe.

Falconer was, however, clear that the UK would be keen to join a future EU defence fund. Last year, negotiations on Britain’s entry into the EU’s €150bn (£125bn) defence fund collapsed after a dispute over the UK’s contribution.

The UK had been seeking to join the first round of the EU’s Security Action for Europe (Safe) fund, a low-interest loan scheme that is part of the EU’s push to grow defence spending by €800bn.

EU officials suggested an entry fee of up to €6bn, significantly higher than the administrative fee the government had anticipated, and talks collapsed. “I think it’s a real shame that the UK was not part of that scheme,” Falconer said on Monday.

“The terms on which we were asked to join were clearly not terms that we could agree and I think that’s a shame – and I think lots of our friends in the EU think it was a shame … so I hope there is an opportunity for a Safe2 and one in which the UK could join on reasonable terms.”

Falconer also said he hoped negotiators would agree a youth mobility scheme ready for the 20 November summit, though there have been crucial sticking points over demands for home tuition fees for EU students at UK universities.

The Guardian reported last week that the UK was prepared to spend up to £450m a year to give EU students discounted university tuition fees, if Brussels allowed Britain access to the Made in Europe industrial programme.

“Obviously I understand that there are a number of member states who would like to see it made easier for EU students to come to the UK and have lower fees than they currently pay if they want to go to British universities,” Falconer said on Monday.

“But that is outside of the common understanding so it will require a different set of arrangements than were envisaged when these negotiations started. Clearly it’s something that on the EU side there’s real interest, so we are engaging in that discussion but I can’t comment in detail on the negotiations.”

Falconer was firmly positive about the UK’s return next year to the Erasmus+ student exchange scheme, which the UK left under Boris Johnson’s Brexit deal in 2020, and will be open not just to universities but also to schools and colleges.

“I hope around the start of the summit we will be opening applications for British young people to put themselves right in the heart of Europe,” he said, rejecting Nigel Farage’s criticisms of Erasmus and the youth mobility scheme as opening the door to uncontrolled immigration.

“People understand that it is a good thing for our young people to be able to travel widely in Europe, to be able to study, to have the kind of exchanges that make such a big difference to people’s lives.

“I think honestly Nigel Farage is being a big old grinch about this. This is in the interests of our young people and he should give his head a bit of a wobble, I think.”

#104. California police investigate after 27 fall ill at half-marathon running event

Thousands took part in the event on a hotter-than-usual day in Santa Barbara

Police in Santa Barbara, California, are investigating after nearly 30 people had to seek medical care at a half-marathon running event held in the city on Sunday.

In a statement provided to the BBC's US partner CBS News, officers said they received a report from Santa Barbara Cottage Hospital about race participants who sought medical treatment "for unusual symptoms" after the race ended.

"The cause of the reported symptoms has not been confirmed," the statement added.

Cottage Health, which operates the hospital, told CBS it had seen "a larger than expected number of emergency visits" from those who ran the race.

"There is no specific action recommended for the public at this time; however, participants who feel unwell should seek medical attention and follow guidance from healthcare professionals," police said.

Cottage Hospital said it treated 27 people from the half marathon at emergency rooms in Santa Barbara and the nearby city of Goleta.

The vast majority of them - 24 - were discharged after treatment, while three people were still in hospital as of Monday in stable condition.

Several thousand people ran the half marathon on a hotter-than-usual day in Santa Barbara along the Pacific Ocean. Temperatures reportedly reached 86F (27C) by midday, compared to a typical early November temperature of low 70s.

One of the runners who went to hospital told local news outlet KEYT-TV that they had lost consciousness and could not remember parts of the race.

"It is awfully suspicious," said the runner, who declined to be identified. "We had people saying they were hallucinating."

"I am not too familiar with heat exhaustion but I have never seen anyone not remembering a long portion of their run," the runner added.

Event director David Monico told US media that 15 people were taken from the medical tent to local hospitals because of dehydration and overheating.

The BBC has contacted the Santa Barbara Police Department, Cottage Health and the half marathon organisers for further comment.
