#103. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.

#101. French police raid Ritz hotel in Mohamed Al Fayed sexual abuse investigation

Arrests made after three properties were searched in relation to inquiry into late businessman

French police have made arrests after raiding three properties, including the Ritz hotel in Paris, in relation to an investigation into sexual abuse and trafficking by Mohamed Al Fayed.

Several individuals were taken into custody for questioning last month and later released, according to the Paris public prosecutor’s office. Another property in Paris and one in Saint-Tropez, both with links to Fayed, were also searched in mid-October.

Several of Fayed’s alleged victims were also interviewed by officers from the organised crime division of the Paris prosecutor’s office.

It added that the searches were part of a preliminary investigation into “allegations of human trafficking committed by an organised group, aggravated pimping committed by an organised group, and rape”.

In an email sent to survivors of Fayed’s alleged abuse earlier today, the Metropolitan police said its officers were present during the searches on 13 and 14 October, in a coordinated operation with French investigators.

The Paris prosecutor’s office said: “Searches and evidence-gathering operations were carried out in mid-October 2026, notably within the Hotel Ritz in the 1st arrondissement of Paris, as well as at the Villa Sainte-Thérèse in Saint-Tropez, a former property of Mohamed Al Fayed, and at the Villa Windsor in the 16th arrondissement of Paris, in the presence of British investigators.”

The investigation is ongoing and digital material seized during the searches is being analysed, it added.

One of the properties that was raided was Castel Sainte-Thérèse in Saint-Tropez, one of the most exclusive villas on the French Riviera, and where Diana, Princess of Wales, stayed with her sons in the summer of 1997, at the start of her relationship with Fayed’s son, Dodi.

The other was Villa Windsor, a former holiday home of the Fayed family. The property in western Paris was previously the family home of Charles de Gaulle, before he was president of France, and King Edward VIII, after his abdication, and his wife, Wallis Simpson.

A Met spokesperson confirmed its officers travelled to France last month and were present during the operation.

Campaign groups representing Fayed’s alleged victims called on the Met police to conduct its own searches of the late billionaire’s properties.

Co-chairs of Justice for Fayed and Harrods Survivors, Jen Mills and Lindsay Mason, said: “We want to see the same thoroughness applied to every property where survivors say they were taken or abused, including here in Britain. The searches in Paris and Saint-Tropez must be the beginning, not the end.”

A spokesperson for No One Above, a collective of survivors of Fayed’s alleged abuse, said: “The French authorities have put this in the hands of their specialist trafficking unit and their organised crime prosecutors, and are investigating it as trafficking by an organised gang.

“The evidence in this case was never confined to Harrods. For two years, the Met has avoided treating it as what France recognised from the outset: organised trafficking by a network. The National Crime Agency should now take over the investigation.”

Pelham Spong, who says she was trafficked in Paris on Fayed’s orders in 2008 before being sent to London to be sexually assaulted by him, said she was overjoyed by the French police raids. “Survivors deserve actions, not words,” she added.

Last November, she told French investigators from the Central Office for the Repression of Human Trafficking that Fayed, who owned the Ritz hotel in Paris, had forcibly kissed her and offered her a salary of €65,000 a year if she slept with him.

Survivors’ groups say more than 500 women have come forward about abuse that allegedly took place at Harrods, Park Lane, Fulham football club, the Ritz hotel in Paris, and other places owned by Fayed. The Met is investigating 169 victims who have contacted the force directly.

The Ritz Paris told Agence France-Presse news agency that it “does not tolerate any form of harassment, abuse or inappropriate behaviour” and that it was ready “to cooperate fully with the competent judicial authorities”.

#99. Iran rejects French claims it was behind supermarket fridge hacking

Shelves were left bare by the cyber-attacks

Iran has described French accusations that it was behind cyber-attacks that raised the temperatures of supermarket fridges and freezers as "baseless and hostile".

On Monday, the French authorities blamed Iran's Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) for the string of hacks, which foreign minister Jean-NoÃ«l Barrot said were "unprecedented" in terms of their scale and "level of aggression".

He added that those responsible would be hit with sanctions.

The French cyber-attacks, which led to the destruction of tons of frozen and chilled food, were followed by a similar hacks against supermarkets across several other European countries. It is not known if the instances are linked.

Barrot said the attack on France's supermarkets "marks the first large-scale cyber-attack aimed at sabotage on our national territory, and it constitutes a major attack on the nation's interests and the security of the French people".

He said the government had summoned the Iranian ambassador to Paris to "lodge a very strong protest against this extremely grave act".

France will take "any other necessary and proportionate measures" to respond to the incident and "deter anyone who might seek to repeat it", Barrot added.

The Paris prosecutor, Laure Beccuau, said an investigation had been opened into the head of an offensive cyber unit associated with the IRGC as well as his two deputies.

Beccuau named the unit as Shahid Kaveh, which she said had an affiliation with the electronic and cyber centre of the IRGC.

Shahid Kaveh is the name given to a cluster of hackers thought to be directly employed by the Iranian regime.

In recent weeks, a number of commentators and politicians in Iran have argued that the country should extend any retaliation beyond the Middle East if the US and Israel resume the war they started earlier this year.

On Monday, some Iranian media outlets denied the French accusations, but there had been no official comment until Tuesday's statement from Iran's foreign ministry.

On 11 October, hackers gained control of the software that regulates the temperatures of the refrigeration units used in many of France's supermarkets.

More than 100 of CoopÃ©rative U's supermarkets were hit, as well as 180 stores from IntermarchÃ© and its Netto brand. In some stores, temperatures of the fridges reportedly rose to 50C (120F) overnight.

Other major food retailers including Carrefour, Monoprix and E.Leclerc were also hit by the attack, which targeted software provided by Danish engineering firm Danfoss.

The software allows retailers to manage their cold units remotely.

Earlier this month a second wave of hacks took place, hitting more than 100 stores across Europe.

Supermarkets in Belgium and Luxembourg were affected, while a smaller number of shops were hit in countries including Sweden, Ireland, the Netherlands, Estonia and Finland.

Separately in August a small number of US military bases had fridges mysteriously malfunction, spoiling food. This incident is also being investigated as deliberate cyber-attacks, Reuters news agency has reported, citing sources familiar with the matter.

Since the waves of attacks on freezers, experts have been speculating which hacking groups could be responsible, with Iran being on a list of potential suspects.

In spite of a pair of Russian-aligned hacktivist groups claiming they were responsible on Wednesday, few believed them.

The French government has not said how it reached the conclusion that Iran was responsible, and attribution of cyber-attacks is extremely hard as hackers are able to hide their locations.

Additional reporting by Farshad Bayan, BBC Persian.

#95. Mortgage holders’ confidence crashes to lowest level since 1994 as year that ‘feels like a recession’ looms

Westpac survey results signal growing sense of ‘helplessness’ while Jim Chalmers faces growing pressure to rein in spending

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Confidence among mortgaged households has crashed to its lowest level in more than three decades amid a growing sense of “helplessness” about the economy, even as a dramatic shift in financial markets suggests there is now a strong chance of a Reserve Bank rate cut by this time next year.

The latest Westpac survey showed its consumer sentiment index sinking again in November to reach a four-year low, amid deepening pessimism about the near-term prospects for the economy.

As Labor comes under increasing pressure over persistent cost-of-living pressures, the prime minister, Anthony Albanese, played down tensions with the treasurer, Jim Chalmers.

A day after the frontbencher Murray Watt called for colleagues to stop undermining Chalmers, Albanese said: “The treasurer is doing a great job”.

The PM said he was working closely with Chalmers in the lead-up to next month’s mid-year budget update, which will include cuts to government spending as pressure mounts to contain inflation.

“This is a treasurer who’s produced a couple of budget surpluses, lower deficits, [who is] producing economic reform,” Albanese said.

Matthew Hassan, Westpac’s head of economic forecasting, said the mood among households was last this bad in 2022, when inflation was running close to 8% and after the RBA had lifted borrowing costs by 4 percentage points over the previous year.

“Prior to that, readings this weak had only ever been seen in the economy’s darkest moments: at the onset of the Covid pandemic; the height of the GFC; and during the recessions in the early 1990s and early 1980s,” Hassan said.

Hassan said while the economic backdrop was not as extreme now as it was during the pandemic and its immediate aftermath, there were “shades of Covid in the responses” from households.

Confidence in the economic outlook over the coming year reached its weakest since August 2020, the survey showed, matching a low point last recorded during the pandemic.

“The comparison with August 2020 is quite useful as that was when Melbourne went into its second lockdown and just before the government massively scaled up income support,” Hassan said.

“There was a sense of helplessness at that stage; there was nothing we could do to avoid the pandemic, and there was nothing the government could do [to avoid the pandemic]. Obviously when the jobkeeper measures were put in place that comforted a lot of people.

“There is a similar feeling of helplessness now. You can argue a substantial portion of what is happening is out of our control, such as rising fuel prices, and there’s only so much the authorities can do to cushion Australians from that impact.”

Indebted households were gloomier still, the survey showed, with pessimism among this group at its worst in records stretching back to 1994.

The latest survey comes a week after Michele Bullock, the RBA governor, warned that the year ahead would “feel like it’s a recession” thanks to persistent cost-of-living pressures alongside weak economic growth.

After five rate increases this year, nearly three in four respondents said they expected another rate hike over the coming 12 months.

Even as the mood in the community darkens, financial markets have begun factoring in the chance of lower interest rates from the middle of next year.

The implied probability of an RBA rate cut was more than 50% by August next year, according to ANZ data, and a decrease to 4.6% was fully priced in by December 2027.

Investors have been rethinking their forecasts for central bank moves alongside a rapid retreat in the global oil price.

After testing a post-Iran war high of nearly US$118 a barrel at the end of October, the global benchmark, Brent crude, has plunged by 20% to below US$94.

“Our base case is that rates do start to come down from August next year, and we don’t see any further hikes,” Hassan said.

#94. Trump says FBI investigating Nvidia boss's affair with alleged Chinese spy

"He's suffering": Trump answers BBC question about Nvidia's Jensen Huang

US President Donald Trump has said the FBI is investigating the affair between the chief executive of Nvidia and an alleged Chinese spy.

Trump said he spoke to Jensen Huang on Monday following reports that the US believed he had been targeted in a Chinese espionage campaign.

"I asked him that question, 'Did you have any idea she was a spy?' He said 'I had no idea she was a spy' and I really believe him," Trump told reporters.

Beijing has rejected claims that Huang was targeted, with its foreign ministry saying the allegations were "entirely groundless, speculative and intended to smear and malign China".

Speaking later at a White House press briefing, Trump told reporters, "I know the FBI is looking into" the affair but did not give further details on the investigation, or confirm when it was launched.

Trump added he thought that Huang was an "honourable guy and I feel very confident that he will be in good shape" after the investigations.

The FBI declined to comment when contacted by the BBC.

Nvidia, which has commissioned an independent investigation into the affair involving Alice Huang, alleged to be a Chinese spy, said it had no reason to believe the company's secrets or national security were compromised.

Nvidia is the world's second most valuable company after Apple, and designs computer chips that have become central to AI technology.

The revelations knocked about $250bn (Â£190bn) off Nvidia's value on Monday as the company's share price fell by more than 4%.

US intelligence officials believe Alice Huang, also known as Huang Le, was a spy for Beijing, according to a New York Times report. She is not related to Jensen Huang.

According to the newspaper, Secretary of State Marco Rubio sent a memo to top officials in Washington in spring this year, warning that the Nvidia boss was in a close relationship with a Chinese spy.

What we know so far about Jensen Huang's affair with suspected Chinese spy

Nvidia shares tumble over investigation into Huang's affair with alleged Chinese spy

Alice Huang was managing director of Singaporean company Megaspeed, which has had dealings with Nvidia.

Trump's remarks are the first indication of whether Nvidia's Huang knew about the woman's alleged ties to Chinese espionage.

Megaspeed's office in Singapore's central business district appeared to be operational and staffed on Tuesday.

The BBC was refused entry to its floor, but spoke to a staff member by phone who said they had no comment and directed all queries to the company's PR team.

The BBC has emailed Megaspeed several times for comment.

Nvidia, co-founded by Jensen Huang, is also a major focus in the AI technology race between the US and China, with officials in Washington treating its exports to Beijing as a matter of national security - a key reason why Huang's affair has sparked concerns.

Also on Monday, Huang and Nvidia were sued in a proposed class action lawsuit in California that accuses them of defrauding shareholders by concealing his ties with the suspected Chinese spy.

The lawsuit alleges that the company lied to the media about Jensen Huang's relationship with Alice Huang, "who is suspected of seeking to help smuggle Nvidia products to China."

The BBC has contacted Nvidia for comment on the lawsuit.

At the White House, Trump was asked several times when he was made aware of the relationship. He said he was informed "over a period of time" but was not specific.

"I hope there's no problem, I believe he is suffering greatly," the president added.

He said he spoke to Huang on the phone on Monday "just to see if he's feeling alright, how you doing and I asked him about that and he said he absolutely had no idea that the person in question was a spy".

Trump has raised concerns that allowing China to lead in AI would supercharge Beijing's military and surveillance capabilities.

The president added he was "sure" Chinese leader Xi Jinping would "ask me about it" when they next met.

Watch: Who is suspected Chinese spy at centre of Nvidia affair investigation?

Huang has admitted that he was "unfaithful" to his wife and apologised for hurting her and their children.

He said on Monday that he was "committed to protecting Nvidia and supporting America's national security" and would cooperate with the investigation the company had launched.

Huang has also been requested to appear for a joint closed-door briefing for several Congressional committees next week, however it is unclear whether Huang will appear.

A letter said the briefing would be "on Nvidia's security protocols against foreign adversary espionage and your interactions with a publicly reported People's Republic of China (PRC) intelligence asset: Megaspeed executive Alice Huang".

Nvidia has declined to comment, while the the BBC has also contacted the committees.

Zoe Kleinman: Nvidia CEO's affair will cause ripples across US and beyond

AI stocks climb after trillions wiped from market values

Some workers the BBC spoke to at Nvidia's Santa Clara headquarters on Monday said it was business as usual, despite the intense attention on the company.

Nvidia has been around for decades, with Huang founding it with two other engineers in 1993, but its rise to one of the world's most valuable and powerful companies has only been recent.

It originally designed computer chips for gaming, but its decision to shift focus to AI development turned it into the powerhouse it is today.

Its chips have become so valuable to the AI buildout that the White House previously imposed controls on where its advanced products could be sold.

The H200, Nvidia's second most advanced semiconductor, had previously been restricted over concerns that it would give China's technology industry an edge over the US.

But Huang has long been against restricting sales to China, arguing that it would be better for the global rollout of AI to be powered by US technology.

In January, the Trump administration said it would allow the company to sell its H200 chips to Chinese customers under certain conditions.

Nvidia's most advanced AI semiconductors including the GB200 Blackwell super chip remain blocked from the Chinese market.

Additional reporting by Tessa Wong in Singapore

#92. Beau Lamarre-Condon admits entering Jesse Baird’s house when he wasn’t home and eating Easter egg, murder trial hears

Former police officer tells jury he joked about being ‘white girl crazy’ with friends as lawyer questions him about start of relationship with TV presenter

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Beau Lamarre-Condon has denied misusing his authority as a police officer to access CCTV footage of Jesse Baird or stalking him after their breakup, a court has heard.

But he admitted he entered Baird’s house while he wasn’t home to retrieve belongings, and ate an Easter egg he had bought for the television presenter.

The former police officer is accused of fatally shooting Baird and his new partner, Luke Davies, with his police service weapon in Baird’s Paddington home in Sydney on 19 February 2024.

He has pleaded not guilty to two counts of murder and one charge of aggravated break and enter.

Sitting in the witness box for the fourth day, Lamarre-Condon was asked about CCTV footage of Baird at the Royal Paddington hotel that was found on the former officer’s iCloud account.

During cross-examination on Tuesday, the crown prosecutor Brett Hatfield asked: “You were stalking Jesse Baird and also misusing your authority as a policeman to access CCTV of him, weren’t you?”

But Lamarre-Condon denied this, telling the court he had been working in Sydney’s city centre on 19 May 2023 when Baird invited him to the Royal Paddington hotel.

He said he had bumped into other officers, whose names he didn’t know, at the pub that day investigating a possible assault.

Lamarre-Condon was looking at the venue’s CCTV to see if it captured anyone doing an intentional or unintentional “shoulder charge”, when he saw Baird enter the premises on the screen.

“I remember filming from my camera,” the former officer told the court.

“I did send it to Jesse then made a joking banter of it [like] ‘I didn’t get to see you, but detective Beau’s on the case’, and sent it to him.”

Hatfield alleged this was a “deliberately false explanation” but Lamarre-Condon disagreed.

Earlier, Lamarre-Condon was shown screenshots he had taken of Baird’s Instagram story after their breakup in April 2023, before Hatfield asked: “Were you stalking Jesse online and in person in this period in May and June 2023?”

Lamarre-Condon responded: “Mr Crown, if you’re going to accuse me of stalking you’re going to have to show my phone records of where I was on these dates.”

“I definitely wasn’t stalking him, I was looking online,” he told the NSW supreme court.

Lamarre-Condon repeatedly said he was the one who had initiated the split, and told the jury this first breakup with Baird was “amicable”.

“We always followed each other,” he told the court. “That’s not stalking. Am I looking at what he posts? Am I a follower? Yes.”

But the court heard he was still upset about the breakup.

“Jesse and I had good times together,” he said. “I did miss that and I miss him as a friend as well, not just as a sexual partner.”

After the split, Lamarre-Condon went to Baird’s home to retrieve his belongings, he told the court.

The former officer claimed he had sent a message that day to ask whether he was going to be home but said: “As I was arriving, he was leaving. I did not ask permission there and then.”

Lamarre-Condon used a key which had been left out to get his clothes, a Polaroid camera and ate an Easter egg he had bought Baird.

“I didn’t steal the Polaroid camera, I purchased that with my money for us to use … that’s not stealing,” he said.

The crown prosecutor referred to previous evidence from Lamarre-Condon’s friend Georgia Cook, who said the former officer told her had been travelling to Baird’s house in his mum’s car and watching the 26-year-old before stealing the camera and consuming the egg.

Lamarre-Condon claimed she had added her own “salt and pepper” to the account.

On Tuesday morning, former officer told the jury about the early days of the relationship, saying he had met up with Baird in October and November 2022 for sexual encounters before the pair began going on romantic dates in December of that year.

Hatfield asked Lamarre-Condon: “You were obsessed with him right from the start?”

Lamarre-Condon denied this, telling the jury: “Jesse Baird could have been a garbage cleaner … I liked Jesse for who he was as a person.”

Hatfield then asked if Lamarre-Condon was “obsessed” with Baird in February and March 2023, the accused responded: “I don’t agree with ‘obsessed’. Smitten? Yes. Obsessed? No.”

Hatfield pointed to messages between Lamarre-Condon and his friends sent around the time the pair began going on dates, in which the officer says: “it sounds crazy, I love him.”

Lamarre-Condon responded: “I say ‘I love you’ to all my friends and to people like that all the time.”

Asked whether he was insecure about the relationship and whether Baird liked him, Lamarre-Condon said his response was “natural” and sometimes occurred when the TV presenter did not reply to his messages straight away.

“I would always joke [with my friends], ‘We’re white girl crazy’,” Lamarre-Condon told the jury.

“It means we should stop being in our head and second-guessing ourselves.”

Lamarre-Condon told the court he had looked through Baird’s phone and used it to unfollow people when the pair went on a brief holiday to a cabin in regional NSW.

“Our relationship was progressing, I just kind of wanted to see where he was at … if he was also messaging other guys romantically … I’m not making excuses for my behaviour.”

Later, when the prosecution attempted to go through messages between Lamarre-Condon and one of his friends, the officer directly addressed Hatfield, telling him: “Mr Crown, I think you’re trying to mislead me here.”

Justice Natalie Adams interjected, directing Lamarre-Condon to “just answer the question”.

“If the crown is misleading you, [the defence lawyer] will object,” she said.

#91. 'Cruise for life' ship seized in Singapore

The arrest comes weeks after the cruise came to a sudden end, leaving 200 guests stranded in Cambodia

A round-the-world cruise that left passengers stranded in Cambodia after its US-based operator ran out of money has been seized in Singapore.

The Odyssey, which set off from Belfast in October 2024 and was meant to take guests on a years-long voyage around the world, is now anchored in Singapore port waters, local authorities say.

Around 200 guests were forced to leave the ship last month in the Cambodian port city of Sihanoukville, after its owner Villa Vie Residences abruptly suspended operations citing "extraordinary increases in fuel costs".

The crew onboard are reported to be well, Singapore's port authority said.

"Arrangements for their welfare and wellbeing are being managed by the vessel's creditors," the authority said, adding that the vessel had been "placed under arrest pursuant to a court order".

Singapore law firm Helmsman LLC has been named the "arresting solicitor", but it is not clear which party it represents.

The Odyssey was meant to go on a three-and-a-half-years voyage covering 150 countries.

Passengers were given the options of buying their cabin outright, which allows them to stay onboard beyond its initial voyage, paying for a five-year residency, or renting a cabin for days-long tours.

Permanent residencies in its cabins ranged from $120,000 (Â£89,272) to $440,000. Those cabins were also available for rent from $3,500 a month, according to its website.

"For roughly the price of a car, you can own your home on a ship that travels the world," its founder Mikael Petterson had earlier said.

However the cruise was beset with problems from the start, with its departure delayed for four months because of repairs and paperwork.

While in Vietnam, crew members reportedly bought food from local grocery stores because the company owed money to a vendor who refused to deliver food, according to CBS News, the BBC's partner in the US.

CBS also reported that the operator warned passengers of its financial troubles in August, when it had sailed on a separate occasion into Singapore, and had to raise money from guests to keep the cruise going.

It had also mentioned the risk of being detained then.

Holly Hennessey, one of the ship's passengers, had earlier told the BBC guests were asked to consider paying a fuel surcharge as prices soared, and that their itinerary had been "severely curtailed".

The vessel sailed again into Singapore earlier this month, weeks after the cruise came to a sudden end while in Cambodia.

As the Iran war sent fuel prices soaring earlier this year, monthly fuel cost per cabin in parts of Asia had risen to as much as $8,000 from $1,800, Villa Vie Residences said in a statement on 27 October.

It added that it had taken "significant steps to reduce fuel consumption" but those measures turned out to be insufficient to offset the "extraordinary increase in fuel costs".

The company also said it was working with residents to "identify temporary living arrangements" while in Cambodia.

#89. Australia to introduce mandatory reporting after OpenAI hack to combat artificial intelligence ‘going rogue’

Government flags criminal law changes to ensure companies can’t argue in future ‘that someone didn’t know the computer was loaded’

Get our daily political email, free app or daily news podcast

OpenAI will not face criminal penalties over the Medicare data breach, but the prime minister has flagged changes to Australia’s criminal law to crack down on future breaches by artificial intelligence companies.

Anthony Albanese on Tuesday released the recommendations of a “rapid review” into the June breach of a Medicare statistics reporting service portal administered by Services Australia, but not the findings or the report itself, which he committed to releasing in the future.

A key recommendation is for the Attorney-General’s Department, the Australian federal police and home affairs to consider introducing “penalties for egregious regulatory non-compliance” and a “failure to prevent” offence, which would criminalise developing an AI model that engaged in harmful conduct while failing “to take reasonable steps to prevent that harm”.

The rapid review came in response to an AI agent, developed by OpenAI, accessing data from Services Australia and three other systems in June. The company did not notify the Australian government until mid-September despite becoming aware of the incident itself in August, prompting criticism from Labor and Albanese.

Guardian Australia revealed last month OpenAI used AI to help write the email to notify Australian authorities.

Sign up for Guardian Australia’s Politics, really newsletter here

Albanese said the government would set up a new AI safety regulator and implement six key obligations on AI companies:

Maintaining security controls across all stages of development and deployment.

Identifying and managing serious risks associated with their models.

Providing mandatory notification of incidents to the Australian government rapidly.

Allowing independent evaluation of their models.

Maintaining and publishing safety and security frameworks to ensure good governance, risk management and transparency.

Responding to updated standards and rules as technologies change.

The review recommended the federal government “consider mandatory, time-bound AI safety reporting”, including around AI agents behaving in ways their creators may not have intended.

The home affairs minister, Tony Burke, appearing with Albanese, told reporters: “It should never be the case that there’s an argument that someone didn’t know the computer was loaded.”

“We need to make sure that our laws and our systems keep pace with the technology and also make sure that we’re able to use the technology to find vulnerabilities before others do,” Burke said.

“The mandatory reporting, when artificial intelligence goes rogue, gives us a chance to be able to make sure that when this happens, the reporting occurs and occurs immediately.”

While Albanese did not directly answer a question about whether the OpenAI incident in June would have breached the proposed new laws if they were in place, government sources indicated that the new legislation would address similar future incidents.

“The clear indication is that no information was got that involves people’s personal details,” Albanese said.

“All of that was confirmed essentially … but that doesn’t mean that it wasn’t a serious issue and that in other areas there might be more serious implications.”

Our political team brings you up to speed on the stories that really matter and what they mean for you

In a statement, an OpenAI spokesperson said the company was reviewing the recommendations and the government’s proposals.

“We have been in close dialogue with the government and we’ll continue to engage constructively as it develops its proposed legislation,” they said.

A separate OpenAI-led process, which is discussing better coordination between AI developers and the government, is expected to report back by 31 December.

The rapid review made 18 recommendations. Albanese said “we accept all of the recommendations and we’ve commenced work to implement them”.

The AI researcher Toby Walsh said the people in charge of AI companies needed to be criminally liable.

“We’ve tried fining them and we’re not the only ones. These companies are incredibly wealthy and it’s almost impossible to fine them enough,” Walsh said.

The Greens leader, David Shoebridge, branded Tuesday’s announcement a “capitulation” to the AI industry, claiming the reforms would not do enough to change behaviour. He said the government should have committed to implementing stronger changes rather than the recommendation to “consider” them.

“Safety keeps coming second to corporate investment, and this review locks that priority in by tying every AI safety effort to bringing in more AI money and more datacentres,” Shoebridge claimed.

“Being a world leader in AI doesn’t mean being a doormat to big tech. It means being prepared to regulate it, to protect Australians and to make sure corporate profits don’t come at the expense of everyone else.”

The shadow home affairs minister, Jonno Duniam, said the Coalition backed the announcement. He said “AI is too important to become an unnecessarily partisan political contest”, signalling an “enduring and multi-partisan approach” to the technology.

#87. DR Congo sentences 66 soldiers to death for being 'cowards'

The Congolese army has been fighting rebels for decades

At least 66 soldiers have been sentenced to death in the Democratic Republic of Congo for cowardice, mutiny and refusing to obey orders, local media report.

The sentences followed a trial of 71 soldiers before a military court in Walikale, in North Kivu province, in eastern DR Congo. Four soldiers were acquitted, while another was sentenced to 10 years in prison.

The army said the trial was intended to improve discipline among troops as they battle armed groups in eastern DR Congo.

The region has endured decades of conflict, with fighting continuing between government forces and the Front for the Refoundation of the Nation (FRN), a rebel coalition that includes the M23 group.

The soldiers were reportedly arrested on 20 October in Walikale, where they were accused of abandoning their positions and fleeing during clashes with FRN fighters.

The Congolese army's campaign against armed groups, including the M23, has long been undermined by poor discipline and disorganisation within its ranks.

The government has repeatedly accused neighbouring Rwanda of supporting the M23, an allegation denied by both Kigali and the rebels despite overwhelming evidence.

The army has faced persistent criticism over its lack of professionalism and disciplined, while soldiers have complained of poor pay and inadequate equipment.

Last month, another 27 soldiers were sentenced to death on similar charges of cowardice after reportedly withdrawing from the frontline during clashes with rebels.

In March 2024, DR Congo lifted a 20-year moratorium on the death penalty, citing the need to remove "traitors" within the army.

The government said capital punishment would also apply to people involved in armed groups, insurrection, as well as committing treason and war crimes.

The decision to reinstate the death penalty was widely criticised by rights groups. However, no formal executions have been carried out since the moratorium was lifted.

Since the beginning of 2025, at least 507 soldiers have been convicted, including 381 death sentences, according to figures compiled by French broadcaster RFI.

In October 2025, former Congolese President Joseph Kabila was sentenced to death in absentia on charges including war crimes and treason. He denied the accusations, but did not appear in court to defend himself. He has since been named as the FRN's leader.

#85. David Jones’s finances were reviewed and restructuring flagged months before it fell into administration

Department store group kept buying new products and fell months behind in payments to some brands despite discussions with administrators

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

David Jones’s administrators have revealed they reviewed its finances six months ago and discussed restructuring the retailer three months before it fell into administration.

The new timeline raises questions over the department store group’s decision to keep buying new products and fall months behind in payments to some brands before it revealed last Thursday it could not pay its debts.

The process could leave unsecured creditors with substantial losses, with unpaid product suppliers and shoppers holding unredeemed gift vouchers most at risk.

The McGrathNicol administrators invited David Jones’s creditors, which also include employees and landlords, to attend a 17 November meeting in its first circular on Tuesday.

McGrathNicol’s team said it had been engaged by the lawyers for David Jones’s owners, Anchorage Capital, as early as April 2026 to analyse some of the department store group’s financial information, though it said it did not directly advise the group or review its ability to pay debts.

By July, David Jones’s group director, Simon Woodhouse, and its biggest lender, Hilco, met McGrathNicol to discuss David Jones’s financial performance, “potential restructuring” and to form a working group to regularly discuss the issues.

But the administrators said Woodhouse left the meeting when the “potential restructuring” was discussed and no working group was formed.

At the same time, David Jones was putting small suppliers on tiered and extended payment terms, with brands forced to accept ever-deeper discounts to get faster payments, according to several businesses contacted by Guardian Australia.

McGrathNicol said it did not discuss David Jones’s dire financial position again until 22 October, when Woodhouse asked them to help plan for potential voluntary administration.

It said Woodhouse and others held another five meetings in the following fortnight before a series of briefings last Wednesday, the day before the administration was announced, as the company negotiated with its landlords and prepared for collapse.

McGrathNicol said David Jones Group paid it $1m upfront that day to run the administration. It told creditors it expected to access another $3m to $4m of funding.

The first creditors’ meeting will take place on 17 November, McGrathNicol said.

A second meeting is typically required six weeks later but McGrathNicol said it would apply to have that delayed another three months, or to early April 2027, to give it more time to sell the business or raise more funds.

The letter did not offer more detail on David Jones’s financial situation, or how long it had been insolvent. McGrathNicol said it had asked the company’s directors to prepare a report on their business, property and financial circumstances.

FTI Consulting, which is running David Jones’s day-to-day business during the administration process, on Monday said the company remained fully operational.

Anchorage Capital, David Jones and Hilco were contacted for comment on Tuesday night.

#82. Police officer dies and another injured after backpack explosion in northern Spain

A police officer has died and another has been injured in the northern Spanish city of Oviedo after an explosive device went off at the scene of a shooting.

The blast happened when officers from Spain's canine unit and explosives disposal unit arrived and examined a suspicious backpack following reports of a dispute between neighbours on Tuesday morning.

A woman who had been shot and the injured officer have been taken to hospital for treatment.

The suspect fled the scene and is still at large, police said.

Spanish police paid tribute to the dead officer, who has been named as Juan JosÃ©, in a statement on X and said: "Our hearts are with his family, friends, colleagues and loved ones."

Officers attended the scene early after receiving reports of an argument between neighbours and upon arriving found a woman who had sustained gunshot wounds, police spokesman Pedro Aguado said.

While searching for the suspected attacker, officers found a suspicious backpack with a flashing light and contacted both the bomb disposal unit and canine unit.

"While the package was being moved outside to ensure the safety of the residents, an explosion occurred," Aguado added.

The explosion left a member of the canine unit dead and an officer from the bomb disposal unit seriously injured, he said.

A three-year-old police dog named Deny was also killed in the explosion.

A widespread police operation is ongoing near the scene of the explosion and officers are continuing to hunt for the suspect.

#79. Victorian Labor’s ‘unforgivable’ preferences decision risks Liberal-One Nation government, Greens warn

Exclusive: Ellen Sandell says Labor’s decision not to back progressives in key inner-seat could hand power to rightwing coalition in close election

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

The Victorian Greens say Labor’s “frankly unforgivable” decision not to preference the minor party in a key inner-city seat could hand the upcoming election to a Liberal-One Nation coalition.

The party’s leader, Ellen Sandell, lambasted Labor’s decision on Tuesday to run an open ticket in Prahran, an inner Melbourne seat currently held by the Liberals’ Rachel Westaway on a tiny 1.3% margin.

It means Labor’s how-to-vote material will encourage voters to place their candidate, Meghan Hopper, first on the ballot but will not direct them how to allocate their remaining preferences. Instead, the remaining boxes on its how-to-vote material will probably remain blank.

Labor preferences are key to the Greens cinching back the seat, which they lost in a 2025 byelection.

The Greens received a 13.4% swing at the poll, with the result influenced by several factors, including Labor’s decision not to field a candidate, low voter turnout, a well-resourced campaign urging voters to put them last and unfavourable preference flows from other candidates. This included the former Labor MP Tony Lupton, who contested the byelection as an independent.

Sandell told Guardian Australia Labor was “showing its true colours” by running an open ticket, as it could inadvertently help the Liberals and One Nation not only secure the seat but also government.

“After months of scaremongering about a Liberal-One Nation government, Labor is now using its preferences to help elect one,” she said.

“This could be one of the most consequential elections in the state’s history. The result may come down to a single seat, and a divisive party like One Nation could hold the balance of power.

“It is frankly unforgivable that Labor would do a dodgy back-room deal to help elect a Liberal-One Nation government – and it’s something that Victorian voters will not easily forget.”

Sandell said Labor voters would be also “shocked” to see the party refusing to preference a “local, progressive Greens woman in Prahran”, Angelica Di Camillo, who also ran at the byelection.

Labor is also running an open ticket in the neighbouring seat of Caulfield, held by the Liberals’ David Southwick on a 2.1% margin. But it is expected Southwick will see a swing in his favour and comfortably retain the seat.

It is understood the local campaigns in Prahran and Caulfield requested the open ticket arrangement, which is consistent with the position Labor took at the 2025 federal election in the overlapping seat of Macnamara, held by Josh Burns, amid the height of the Israel-Hamas war in Gaza.

A Labor spokesperson said: “Our message is simple: put a 1 next to Labor candidates everywhere. We’re fighting for majority government.”

Caulfield is home to Victoria’s largest Jewish community, which also extends into St Kilda East in the Prahran electorate.

On Tuesday, the Liberals and Nationals also confirmed they would exchange preferences with One Nation, with the opposition leader, Jess Wilson, telling reporters how-to-vote cards for Coalition candidates would instruct voters to put Labor, the Greens and the Victorian Socialists “at the bottom of the ticket, and all other parties above that”.

Just hours after Wilson confirmed the position, One Nation moved to disendorse its Preston candidate, Franco Guardiani, over tweets, including one that described Hitler as the “GOAT”, an acronym which stands for greatest of all time.

The Victorian Liberal Senator Jane Hume told ABC’s Afternoon Briefing Guardiani’s tweets were “vile” and confirmed he would be last on the party’s how-to-vote material in the electorate.

#78. Arrests made in Mohamed Al Fayed probe in France, police say

Hundreds of women have come forward with abuse allegations against the former Harrods and Ritz Paris owner

French police have made arrests after three properties, including the Ritz hotel in Paris, were searched in relation to an investigation into Mohamed Al Fayed's abuse.

The Paris prosecutor's office told the BBC that several individuals were taken into custody for questioning last month and later released.

In a confidential letter sent to survivors, the Metropolitan Police said its officers were present during the searches on 13 and 14 October, in what is understood to be a joint operation between British and French authorities.

It is the first time any arrests have been made in relation to alleged abuse and trafficking by the former Harrods and Ritz owner.

Mohamed Al Fayed accused of multiple rapes by staff

Several victims of Al Fayed were also interviewed by French investigators, the prosecutor's office said.

It added that the searches were part of a preliminary investigation into "allegations of human trafficking committed by an organized group, aggravated pimping committed by an organized group, and rape".

The investigation is ongoing and digital material seized during the searches is being analysed.

The late Egyptian billionaire bought the Ritz Paris hotel in 1979, and it is still owned by the Al Fayed family.

The Villa Windsor in western Paris, another property involved in the search, was a holiday home for the Al Fayed family. It was previously the home of King Edward VIII, following his abdication, and his wife Wallis Simpson.

The Met shared this statement from French authorities with victims:

"At this stage of the proceedings, several victims have been interviewed by French investigators, and the investigations are being conducted in liaison with the British authorities.

"In this context, searches and evidence-gathering operations were carried out in mid-October 2026, notably within the Hotel Ritz in the 1st arrondissement of Paris, as well as at the Villa Sainte-ThÃ©rÃ¨se in Saint-Tropez, a former property of Mohamed Al Fayed, and at the Villa Windsor in the 16th arrondissement of Paris, in the presence of British investigators.

"Several suspects were taken into police custody, measures which were lifted following their interviews, specifically for the purpose of analyzing digital material seized during the search operations.

"The investigations are continuing under the direction of the Paris Public Prosecutor's Office."

A Met Police spokesperson confirmed its officers travelled to France in October and were present during the operation.

The Ritz hotel in Paris is still owned by the Al Fayed family

Al Fayed, who died in 2023 aged 94, was accused by more than 20 women of sexual assault in a BBC documentary and podcast in September 2024.

One of the women featured in the documentary said she was sexually assaulted by Al Fayed at the Ritz hotel in Paris, while another woman said she had been raped by him at the Villa Windsor residence.

Since those allegations, more than 400 alleged victims or witnesses have come forward to lawyers.

A woman has also told BBC Scotland she was trafficked, drugged, sexually assaulted and held prisoner on the Balnagown Estate by Al Fayed's brother, Salah Fayed, who died in 2010.

Both brothers died without facing legal charges in relation to these allegations.

Earlier this year, Police Scotland said it was carrying out further investigations into allegations connected to Balnagown Estate.

In a statement, a force spokesperson said: "Three people have been identified in connection with offending against a number of victims.

"Our inquiries are ongoing and it would be inappropriate to comment further."

The Justice for Fayed and Harrods Survivors group said it welcomed the "significant developments" and cooperation between the Met and French authorities.

"We believe that all properties owned by Fayed should be investigated with the same thoroughness as those already searched, wherever they are located, to ensure that no potentially relevant evidence is overlooked," the group said.

"We hope these developments represent meaningful progress towards identifying and holding to account all those who facilitated, concealed or assisted his offending, wherever they operated."

The survivor advocacy group No One Above said: "France is treating this as organised human trafficking, led by specialists, with properties searched and evidence seized. That is what a serious investigation into a network looks like.

"Two years on, survivors are still waiting for the same from the Met. It is time the National Crime Agency took this investigation over."

The management of the Ritz Paris told AFP news agency that it "does not tolerate any form of harassment, abuse or inappropriate behaviour" and that it was ready "to cooperate fully with the competent judicial authorities".

If you have been affected by any of the issues raised in this story, information and support is available at BBC Action Line.

#73. US federal authorities could have prevented Epstein crimes, report finds

Jeffrey Epstein died in a New York prison cell in 2019

Crimes committed by the late disgraced financier Jeffrey Epstein after 2005 could have been prevented were it not for "failures" by federal investigators and prosecutors, a congressional investigation has concluded.

The Republican-led US House Oversight Committee on Tuesday released its final report into what it described as the "decades-long" mishandling of the criminal investigations into Epstein and his long-term associate Ghislaine Maxwell.

The report's findings are based on interviews and depositions with witnesses, government files, and financial records over a 15-month period.

Epstein died in 2019 as he awaited trial on sex trafficking charges.

"This is my way of trying to breathe," says Joanna Harris on speaking about Epstein for first time

"While some of these failures involved an exercise in poor judgement by the USAO, others appeared to be the result of Epstein's pervasive and wide-reaching influence," the report reads, referring to the US Attorney's Office.

It states that, rather than fully investigating allegations in other states, the USAO for the Southern District of Florida entered an agreement with Epstein in 2007 allowing him to evade federal prosecution.

'There is no justice' - Epstein survivor on how model agency sent her to abuser's home

Regarding Epstein's death in a New York prison, investigators concluded this was a result of "a series of institutional failures at the facility," which included staffing shortages, overworked employees, poor recordkeeping, and lax security practices.

Testimony the panel solicited was "unable to definitively dispel certain theories" surrounding Epstein's death, the report states, including accounting for a lapse in surveillance footage.

It found the justice department (DoJ) failed to communicate about document releases and that deficient redactions exposed survivors' sensitive personal information.

The DoJ has released millions of pages of Epstein-related files in response to legislation from Congress.

Earlier this year, the department had to remove thousands of documents related to Epstein from its website after victims said their identities had been compromised.

The report also alleges that financial institutions like American Express enabled human trafficking and immigration fraud, by allowing Epstein to use decoy flights and dummy itineraries to transport foreign workers across borders. The BBC has contacted American Express for comment.

The investigative panel recommended that Congress should pass legislation to prevent the weaponisation of non-disclosure agreements, which they said Epstein used to silence survivors.

"Epstein operated with impunity, shielded and enabled by powerful connections and a justice system that repeatedly failed his victims," said James Comer, the Republican chairman of the committee.

He called upon the DoJ to "interview or re-interview key witnesses, investigate Epstein associates accused of criminal activity, and ensure survivors have access to their records.

The department remains under bipartisan scruntiny regarding the Epstein files.

In the state of New Mexico, where Epstein had a ranch, the top state prosecutor accused the DoJ of stonewalling their criminal investigation.

"With all due respect, the Chairman's recommendations have already been addressed," a spokesperson for the department said in response to Comer's report.

"The Attorney General has repeatedly said that the Epstein case is not closed and there is no statute of limitations on human trafficking."

The report accuses Democrats of being "obsessed" with trying to implicate US President Donald Trump and that an "unfounded witch hunt continued without regard for the interests of survivors".

Trump has previously said he was friendly with Epstein before they fell out in 2004. The president's name has appeared thousands of times in the Epstein files but there is no suggestion that appearing in the documents implies any wrongdoing.

The committee's review of documents did not identify any that would implicate Trump, the report concluded.

In a statement, Rep Robert Garcia, the top Democrat on the Oversight Committee, alleged his Republican colleagues "released a report based on an incomplete investigation and zero public hearings on Jeffrey Epstein".

Garcia accused the justice department of withholding "over 2.5 million pages of documents".

"We will not allow the President and his allies to dismiss this investigation," said Garcia, who is expected to lead the committee when Democrats take control of Congress in January following the recent midterm elections.

The family members of one of Epstein's most prominent accusers, the late Virginia Roberts Giuffre, thanked Comer for releasing Tuesday's report.

"A sentence from the report stands out to us: 'Questions remain as to whether the DOJ has released all its relevant documents.' We could not agree more and urge that all 7 million-plus files be released," Sky and Amanda Roberts said in a statement.

"There are many more individuals who have yet to be identified and brought to justice," they said.

#71. New Zealand election coalition talks will likely take weeks – as it happened

This blog is closed, you can read our report on the New Zealand election here

Thanks for following the blog tonight. The election has ended without a clear winner. Here are the top news lines from what will go down as one of New Zealand’s closest elections in many decades.

Christopher Luxon’s National party and its right-leaning coalition partners are on the brink of a wafer-thin majority that could allow it to eke out a return to power.

The left-wing bloc, led by Labour’s Chris Hipkins, fell short of a majority.

Neither Luxon or Hipkins claimed victory or conceded defeat. Both acknowledged an expected wait before a governing coalition is negotiated.

A centrist newcomer, the Opportunity party, has entered parliament, polling above the 5% threshold. They could potentially be a kingmaker in a coalition.

Final results are expected on 27 November. Coalition talks will likely take weeks.

Hundreds of thousands of special votes are yet to be counted and could change the shape of parliament.

Saturday figures showed there could be a 130-seat parliament, with 66 seats needed for a majority.

Results suggested a coalition of National, Act and NZ First would hold 67 seats.

The 67 included 42 for National, 13 for Act and 12 for NZ First.

The left-bloc totalled 56 seats: Labour 35, Greens 16 and 5 for Te Pāti Māori.

Opportunity is projected to gain 7 seats.

Over the coming days and weeks we will get a sense of the magnitude of Qiulae Wong and the Opportunity party’s success tonight. Opportunity are currently projected to claim seven seats in parliament.

Wong has said that if she held the tiebreaker power, she was open to negotiating with either a conservative or a left-wing government and would deal first with whichever major party won the most votes.

The right-wing bloc might not need her, and Luxon has ruled out governing with Opportunity. Analysts say, however, that both major party leaders would likely answer the phone if Wong held the only path to power.

You can read more about the rise of Opportunity here, in this piece from Michelle Duff.

A couple of pics of Christopher Luxon as he arrived to speak to supporters tonight.

On the current count, the Greens are celebrating a best-ever result of what looks to be 16 seats in parliament.

With 0.56% support, Te Pāti Māori will be stoked with its result tonight, writes Jamie Tahana.

It seems odd, but the Māori party has spent the election deliberately asking people not to give them their party vote. Instead, they asked for electorate votes only.

Just a reminder, seven of the seats in New Zealand’s parliament are reserved for representatives of Māori voters – you can read more about them here.

The party’s co-leaders, Rawiri Waititi and Debbie Ngarewa-Packer, have comfortably won their respective seats of Waiariki and Te Tai Hauauru; Oriini Kaipara swept the Auckland seat of Tamaki Makaurau; Hana Rawhiti Maipi-Clarke, who hit international stardom for her haka in parliament, has cruised across the line in Hauraki-Waikato; while Aperahama Edwards holds a narrow lead over the Greens’ Hūhana Lydon in the far North seat of Te Tai Tokerau.

That’s five seats. But with a party vote so low, they will be five overhang seats.

Still, after a term that has seen plenty of in-fighting in Te Pāti Māori, there are signs of frustration in the Māori electorate, with Waititi and Ngarewa-Packer seeing significantly reduced majorities.

The Greens have fallen short of gaining their first ever Māori electorate, but across the seats party vote has risen to about 40%, making the party a significant force in Māori politics.

Labour, having been punished by the Māori electorate in 2023, has improved, particularly in terms of party vote, and has claimed a new MP in Mananui Ramsden, who won the country’s largest electorate, Te Tai Tonga.

So, hundreds of thousands of special votes are going to potentially change where things stand right now.

In his speech tonight, Luxon said 300,000 special votes were yet to be counted. Those votes, which will be counted and known by 27 November, typically sway left.

In the 2023 election there 600,000 special votes, and National dropped from 50 seats in the preliminary count to 48 seats.

In a 130-seat parliament, in which 66 seats are needed to govern, this is where things stand with 97% of the vote counted. However, special votes are still to be counted and the lay of the land will not be clear until 27 November.

Labour 35, Greens 16, Te Pāti Māori 5 = 56

National 29.3%, Labour 29.2%, Greens 13.3%, Act 11%, NZ First 9.8%, Opportunity 6%, Te Pāti Māori 0.6%.

Jamie Tahana has this hot-take on the Luxon speech:

It was a tough line to tread for a speech: National is the biggest party on current results – 29.30% – but Luxon has also led the party to one of its most unpopular points, only 0.1% ahead of Labour. Luxon has also lost some of his most senior and experienced MPs, which he conceded required “some tough phone calls”.

Luxon said that because of the closeness of the result, and with special votes still to be counted, caretaker conventions will apply.

“For now, I want to tell New Zealanders that well established caretaker conventions apply … The daily business of government will continue.”

Caretaker convention basically means that while a new government is being formed, the incumbent government is in “caretaker” mode, which means it will carry on the normal business of governing.

Jamie Tahana notes that for Luxon to continue as prime minister, he will have to cobble together a three- or four-party coalition, from a weakened position compared to 2023.

In his speech, Luxon said: “Negotiations and compromises are going to be required … A number of races are still too close to call.”

Jamie Tahana has some more from the Luxon speech:

Luxon called the election “too close to call”, and said “meaningful negotiations” for a new coalition government will start after the final results are known on 27 November.

“It looks like an MMP special,” he said. “It’s heartening to see that so far National is once again the largest party in parliament and we have a path to forming the next government.”

Luxon concedes result unlikely tonight, saying that meaningful negotiations will begin after final results on 27 November.

Luxon arrived to a crowd of about 700 raucous supporters chanting “three more years”. He was flanked by his wife Amanda and two children, waved and grinned as he descended a stairwell flanked in blue light.

A heavy music track - which could not be immediately identified through Shazam - thrummed as he walked down a long aisle, hugging supporters.

When he took the podium, Luxon conceded the election was “too close to call” but the crowd cheered as he announced National had secured the highest party count.

He went on to acknowledge Labour leader Chris Hipkins, and admonished supporters who booed Quilae Wong and Opportunity, saying it was “not the kiwi way”.

Luxon is also paying tribute to those high-ranking National MPs who find themselves out of a job tonight: Nicola Willis, Paul Goldsmith and Gerry Brownlee. “I want to thank you … for your years of service to our party”.

Luxon has called tonight’s result “an MMP special” and says “on the numbers so far, National is once again the largest party in parliament”.

It will be interesting to see what Luxon has to say. How will he frame the vote count and National’s performance?

Christopher Luxon is high-fiving and hugging supporters. He’ll be speaking soon.

#68. Twelve dead after motorway pile-ups in Bulgaria fog

Burnt out and crushed vehicles were strew across the motorway in the aftermath of the crash

Twelve people have been killed after dozens of cars and lorries were involved in two pile-ups on the Trakia motorway in south-eastern Bulgaria, Prime Minister Rumen Radev has said.

Health Minister Katya Ivkova said 11 people died at the scene on Tuesday, near the town of Zimnitsa, while another person later died in hospital. About 50 others were taken to hospital with injuries.

Sliven regional governor Mihail Kasherov told journalists that more than 70 vehicles were involved in the incident, Bulgarian state news agency BTA reported.

Officials have said dense fog reduced visibility and was the likely cause of the first crash, which occurred on the eastbound carriageway.

Radev declared Wednesday a day of national mourning following the incident.

The second crash, on the Sofia-bound carriageway, was likely also caused by the fog, with drivers also distracted by the first crash, BTA reported an official as saying.

The Trakia motorway runs from west-to-east of Bulgaria, and connects the capital Sofia to the Black Sea coast.

Road administration director Galin Kostov added that speeds were unsuitable for the conditions, which included wet road surfaces, and the motorway would remain closed until it had been cleared, BTA reported.

Yambol regional governor Georgi Chalakov said reports first came in at 07:10 local time (05:10 GMT), with the injured taken by ambulance to hospitals in the Yambol, Sliven and Burgas regions.

Chalakov told BTA rescue teams had to remove sections of the crash barrier to reach people. He added that the fog meant that helicopters could not be used in the evacuation efforts.

A drone captured the scene from above, just visible through the dense fog

One witness who helped rescue a man from a burning vehicle blamed "very thick fog" for the pile-up.

"I got out of the car, I ran forward and pulled that man out," the witness told Reuters news agency.

Another told Reuters that "many, many people died".

"As for me, there were four cars behind me - trucks - and I don't know how I got out or what happened, but in the end, thank God I'm still alive," that witness said.

Radev - who had been hosting European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen - cut short a news conference after learning of the incident and visited the scene.
